i'm not going to tell you. services account for three quarters of the union's gdp. the productivity of service work grew in the european union at half the rate of the united states between 2000 and 2017. at the same time, there are large differences in productivity growth between member states. over the same period, italian labour productivity fell by four per cent, while estonia's increased by 61 per cent. if we were to raise productivity in services to the level of the three most successful countries in the union, gross domestic product could grow by about 5 percent. what are the responsibilities of the commission and the member states? the commission and the member states should therefore come up with analyses of best practices and motivations for reform. the closest we've gotten is when we evaluated the recovery plans. we should also push for further removal of barriers in the services market. for example, by reducing the number of regulated professions, of which there are some 6 000 in the union. today's restrictions do not protect the consumer from substandard services from other member states, they only protect producers from competition, to the detriment of consumers and the whole union, as well as its members. what producers gain from the safeguard measures chosen is less than what consumers lose. further liberalisation is therefore necessary and desirable for the european union as a whole.
mr president, i would like to thank you for your time. to end the covid-19 pandemic, we need to provide global immunity through a vaccine. we have a vaccine, and now it's crucial that it's available to people all over the world, in all countries, rich and poor. in one year, the pandemic caused astronomical economic damage, estimated at $11 trillion. the vaccine will stop this pandemic, but it won't stop others from coming. almost every pandemic so far has been caused by zoonotic transmission, that is, transfer from animals to humans. what we need to prevent is the process that's associated with the outbreak of these diseases. these are very clearly linked to the destruction of nature and the exploitation of animal species. there are about 1.7 million previously unknown viruses circulating in wildlife populations. half of them have zoonotic potential. if we continue to deforest, to hunt massively, and to trade wildlife, we are critically increasing the risk of further transmission. investing in nature conservation can prevent these diseases. scientific estimates suggest that effective protection would cost about $30 billion a year. the damage caused by the virus would cover effective measures for 500 years. instead of buying mining licenses and importing deforestation, we need to invest in natural areas for the purpose of not mining them. we need to invest in pristine natural areas and ensure that they remain so. strict nature conservation is both an insurance policy and an investment in these steps. it's really not too soon. it's a life.
in writing. the european commission, in collaboration with member state governments, must do everything possible to ensure that vaccines are available to all citizens and, as a priority, to the elderly, the healthcare workers and people at risk throughout the union. it is inconceivable that despite the announced provision of two billion vaccines, there are delays in the delivery of vaccines to some eu countries, jeopardising the target of vaccinating 70% of the population by the summer. if the european commission, on behalf of the eu, has pre-purchase agreements with several vaccine manufacturers and has over-financed their development and production by hundreds of millions, it cannot be that suppliers like pfizer-biontech are already announcing a drop in agreed supplies. it is also necessary to ensure the control of exports of any vaccine doses from the eu that have been produced here. the vaccination process needs to be accelerated all the more as a new, much more infectious mutation of the coronavirus is already spreading in almost all regions of europe. ideally, citizens should be able to choose between vaccines based on mrna technology and so-called adenovirus vector vaccines. as regards the so-called vaccination card, we must honestly answer the question whether we really want to take away the personal freedoms and rights of people who, for various serious reasons, do not want to be vaccinated or cannot objectively be vaccinated. last but not least, in addition to vaccines, adequate efforts should continue to be made to find effective treatments for covid-19.

the reporter. i'm not going to tell you. the current pandemic crisis has serious economic and social consequences for almost everyone. but the people who paid the most for it, in all member states, were those at risk of poverty. these people don't decide if or when they're going on an exotic vacation. these people are deciding whether they can afford to pay for a hot meal for themselves and their children or to smoke in the house. the impact of the pandemic will be a huge increase in unemployment and a drastic reduction in living standards for millions of europeans. but if the standard of living of people who have already started from the poverty line is falling, we need to act quickly and we need to act effectively to save them. and that is how i see the fead, the fund for european aid to the most deprived. it must be fast, targeted, flexible and tangible. in my own country, slovakia, after the first wave of the pandemic, there was a significant increase in the number of households that freeze in winter, literally because they can't pay for heat. almost eight percent of households in slovakia were in a situation where they could not pay their electricity bills after the first wave of the pandemic. this is a huge increase from 2016, when it was 5 percent of such households. currently, 16 percent of families in slovakia, including those with young children, are trapped in poverty. since the outbreak of covid-19, the situation of more than 20% of citizens of the european union at risk of poverty and social exclusion has deteriorated. the fund for european aid to the most deprived is the instrument that helps people in need to escape poverty and social exclusion. through the fund, states can provide food and basic material assistance, but also non-material assistance to meet the basic needs of those most in need. the amount of assigned revenue in accordance with article 21 (3) of the financial regulation is estimated at eur 100000. the crisis has deepened social disparities and inequalities. member states need to draw money from the european aid fund to help the most vulnerable. the crisis has, of course, also had a negative impact on the budgets. i am really pleased that we have agreed on all these important elements, and i firmly believe that this review will ensure that the aid that is so important at this time and in this crisis madam president, the role of the high representative is to interact and engage with his counterparts from other countries, even in difficult times and on problematic topics. but this visit to moscow had an extremely bad timing. despite eu calls on russia to refrain from arresting opposition leader navalny and to stop the harassment of demonstrators, russia does exactly the opposite and the eu is advised to keep its mouth shut. by expelling eu diplomats on the same day as the visit and by publicly stating that the eu is not a reliable partner, russia has confirmed once again that it does not wish to mend the relations with the eu and the eu must build on this knowledge. the level of naivety towards russia in europe should be kept low. russia has made its geopolitical choices for now. hopefully the eu will draw quick conclusions about what happened: among others, the inclusion of human rights violators on the sanctions list and the suspension of nord stream 2, which is contrary to european energy security policy.
madam president, high representative, as a former diplomat, i deeply regret the expulsion of three european diplomats by the russian federation. this decision is completely unfounded. why do we have a vienna convention on diplomatic relations? a diplomat is entitled to assess the developments in the receiving state and to report to the government of the sending state. when diplomats are hindered by a state from objectively observing a democratic protest, profound questions can be posed on the functioning of that state, on the compliance with the rule of law, on the respect for democracy and human rights. our relations with russia are at a low point. a european strategic approach is more than necessary. we must prevent further deterioration, show readiness to respond with sanctions, but also a willingness to rebuild ways towards renewed dialogue. please let diplomacy do its job, because we know what happens when diplomacy ends.
madam president, mr borrell, let what has happened serve as a guide for future dialogues with regimes such as venezuela, cuba, turkey or iran. the obsession of all of them, after all, is to destroy our democratic european system of principles and values. history repeats itself, a colleague of mine said: fascisms and communisms, extreme left and right pulling - and financed by our regimes - here in russia today. there is the vice-president of the government of spain, of my own country, pablo iglesias, financed in the past by venezuela and iran and now acting as a spokesman for the putin regime in its daily task of undermining the prestige of spain and eroding the democratic system that so much effort, sweat and tears cost us. and in the spanish case, of course, not a step backwards in the face of russian interference supporting racist and exclusionary nationalisms in spain and catalonia. mr borrell, we must sit down with the democratic opposition in russia and support them. and to do that, we must resort to the only weapon these gangsters understand: sanctions now. i have heard many colleagues here talk about mr borrell's resignation, but i have heard very little about the sanctions, which are a thousand times more effective. it is no longer necessary to hide behind the european commission. the next european council must make it clear who is in favour and where the unanimity is when it comes to imposing sanctions on russia. and if they are not, they should say so publicly and face the consequences of european democratic public opinion. the time for soft diplomacy is over. it is time for european political leadership, which, together with the new american administration, has the duty to defend our democracies and freedoms.
madam president, mr borrell, a popular russian saying that was heard in moscow this weekend is, in essence, 'whoever is in too much of a hurry is likely to be laughed at'. and unfortunately, it's hard to think of anything else today. the fiasco was announced everywhere, including by us, and the trap of putin and lavrov was cleverly orchestrated. we are still in favour of dialogue, of course. but your visit to moscow did not advance the cause of alexei navalny, nor the cause of human rights in russia. you said, "i listened to you religiously", wanting to address the russians, and you're right, it's essential. that's the whole point of tomorrow. talking to the russians, talking to these young people, thousands of them in the streets and who, deep down, only know putin in power. but i don't see how that mess speaks to them today. one last thing, even though i'm playing devil's advocate in this debate. it is clear that you were caught between the hammer and the anvil, between our ambition, your ambition certainly, and the frivolity of the member states. all this to say that it is the function of high representative which must be reinvented by the conference on the future of europe.
madam president, high representative, it is with regret and a sense of shame that i stand here today speaking of last week’s events. unfortunately, mr borrell, your visit to russia has demonstrated that reaching out to moscow under the current circumstances is not only futile, it is dangerous. the last thing we need to hear from a european politician speaking in moscow today is a criticism of our ally in washington. when i see the frustration of the belarussian and russian people and the unstoppable force of lukashenko and putin’s regimes, i expect from the representative of european diplomacy clear messages about sanctions, political prisoners, disinformation, russia’s dirty money, and its attempts to undermine the european way of life. i want to convey my sincere support to those fighting for democracy and fundamental rights in the streets of minsk, moscow and other cities and towns across belarus and russia. russia is a regime that kills and jails its political opponents. the european union cannot engage with it in a manner of business as usual. and, by the way, our messages to russia also have foreign policy implications for actions in our neighbourhood and in the western balkans. believe me, the fight for the european hearts and minds of our neighbours will be easier with clear red lines towards moscow. i wish sincerely that we did not need to have this debate today for europe’s biggest opponent in our relations with russia sits in moscow. let us not give him any more comfort. let’s stand up to russia’s dark side, united, and in clear defence of europe’s values and interests.
madam president, the contracts that the commission has concluded with the vaccine suppliers should have been made public long ago, as it has been for all government contracts in slovakia for a decade, and nothing terrible has happened. the commission has not received any comments from interested parties on the compatibility of the aid with the internal market. contracts should always be made public, so it's in the interest of the citizen and the public. the treaties could also include a clause on the order in which the union would receive vaccines and adequate sanctions for failure to meet the agreed conditions. i believe that in the future, the european union will be more transparent in any procurement, and this will certainly translate into better results.
madam president, today we are having a difficult but very necessary debate - why is the european union lagging behind in vaccination? yet it would be far worse for the union to debate why some of the member states have gotten all the vaccines while the smaller and poorer ones have got nothing. which is why i still think that the joint vaccine procurement was a good idea. but clearly, she could have had a better time. where's it stuck? if the problem is the human factor, it's easy to solve. but if the problem is caused by the limitations of the european union's structure, then it requires systemic solutions. the question must be asked whether the commission is sufficiently equipped with the expertise and powers to manage procurement? has the decision-making system in the council contributed to the delay? should the ema have approved the vaccine sooner? the last question has a clear answer -- when you vaccinate half a billion people, with vaccines that are developed in record time, bottlenecks and gambling are not an option. a serious defect in any of the approved vaccines would have catastrophic consequences, not only for the current situation, but for the general public's trust in science, vaccination and democracy. in conclusion, i believe there is time to correct the situation and speed up the vaccination. but if, in the fall, the european union were to lock down again because of delays, and the rest of the developed world did not, the consequences could be disastrous.
madam president, the eu’s vaccination strategy is a tale of two starkly different halves. i mean on the one hand it is a testament to the importance of the european union that we have successfully managed to avoid a bidding war between member states. a european battle for vaccines would have had catastrophic consequences, especially for smaller member states like mine, slovakia. on the other hand, it is now quite obvious that the commission has committed a number of serious mistakes in negotiating the contract and managing the supply of vaccines. and pondering an export ban to northern ireland, whatever the technical details, has been a pr disaster that the uk government is now trying to exploit to blame all brexit problems on the eu. i really hope that the commission will learn from these mistakes because the coming weeks and months, and whether we are able to step up production, will be a make—or—break moment for citizens’ trust in the european union.
madam president, i am still in favour of finding better targeted treatments for covid-19, as well as using existing medicines if they have been shown to be at least somewhat effective. but prevention is better than cure, of course, and it's clear that vaccination, while not a miracle cure for a pandemic, is the most effective way to at least contain this dramatic situation, with its hundreds of thousands of deaths and economic crisis. the common european approach to vaccine procurement, which i advocate, was aimed at ensuring rapid and fair distribution to all member states. but the long-running slippage in the supply of the agreed vaccines is causing great frustration among our citizens. we must therefore allow the production of vaccines in all member states that can provide it. we need to get the producers to give up their patents, because they've been getting research and development money from our taxpayers, because otherwise i'm going to explain to our constituents that despite the millions invested in various pharma-firms, they don't agree to relocate production.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, social media have increased opportunities to participate in public life and political debate, facilitating access to information and freedom of expression. but we are still not adequately protected from the proliferation of fake news, discriminatory content, incitement to hatred and violence. we have seen how disinformation campaigns and foreign interference can find fertile ground to influence public opinion, to polarize societies, to undermine democratic processes. with the action plan for democracy and the proposals on digital services and markets, we expect significant action to be taken to regulate the opaque practices of micro-targeting and to achieve greater transparency on the functioning of algorithms in the procedures for monitoring and moderating online content. at the same time, however, it is essential to mitigate the risk of conflict with freedom of opinion arising from decisions taken on the basis of discretionary powers of an oligarchy of private commercial entities, rather than on the basis of rules that provide greater guarantees of freedom and impartiality.
madam president, madam secretary of state, madam commissioner, the public sphere and the media sphere have changed radically. from a world in which we had mediators and mediations (in politics, parliaments, parties, institutions; in the social sphere, the press, television, radio), we have suddenly passed through social networks to the illusion of direct democracy. the mediators have fallen, and now on social media, everyone thinks they can abolish the media and traditional media. everyone thinks that through direct participation they can even replace parliaments. there is no shortage of countries where the abolition of parliaments is decreed or advocated. no more mediators. only direct participation, through social networks, with a vote that even in laws and decisions could be daily through digital tools. the problem is that we suddenly realize that behind social networks and the companies that own them, there is a mediator, a hidden mediator, a secret mediator, who is the algorithm, who is the controller, who is the owner of social networks, and therefore freedom of expression is in danger. it is in danger of outside interference, it is in danger of hidden mediation, it is in danger of lack of democratic regulation. and that's why we can't be in the hands of social media. we have to take them into our own hands. that is the challenge we are debating here today.
madam president, although we are now one year into the pandemic, facebook only decided this week to take down posts claiming vaccines cause autism. it took them one year to act against fake news. and a study released this week reveals that 83% of young people have already been confronted with fake news on social media and 25% of them cannot distinguish between fake news and real news. and this is, in my eyes, a threat. for years, and i think i am not exaggerating when i say social media platforms and trump, for example, have benefited from each other. only after trump’s election defeat and him calling on his supporters to storm the capitol did they decide to ban him. that means that facebook, youtube and twitter took more than four years to ban him. and while i think it was right to ban mr trump from social media platforms, i would say that private companies should never act without reference to the law, and as the european union this is the way we have to go.
madam president, i think it’s now clear that most social media have wildly outgrown their founders’ expectations and their impact on society is felt globally and, of course, in each individual country. with such great power must come greater responsibility, because today there is very little incentive for social media corporations to ensure that their products are not used as vehicles for the mass spread of hate or lies or disinformation. in slovakia, last year, journalists tracked 56 death threats posted publicly on facebook, and actually had to call facebook’s pr department before the death threats were taken down. so it was because of the action of journalists that the threats actually disappeared. this is illustrative of a much bigger problem, which is that the social media corporations are unable or unwilling to ensure that their content not only meets ethical standards, but actually complies with the legislation and the laws of the country in which they operate. so, when we think about the future, the regulation must not be virtual, but real.
madam president, madam minister, madam commissioner, social media platforms such as facebook and twitter have arrogated to themselves the exorbitant power to regulate freedom of expression on the internet. they use it to enforce the law of conformity by ruthlessly censoring all ideas that offend, shock or worry, in violation of the handyside ruling of the european court of human rights which expressly protects them. their dominance has become such that they have even cut off the accounts of the american president, who is considered the most powerful man in the world. this oppression and censorship has lasted too long. the european states and the european union, which is nothing but their delegate, must take back control of the situation. in accordance with the european parliament resolution of 20 october 2020, i call for the strengthening of public scrutiny on these platforms in order to guarantee freedom of expression. i also ask that any decision to remove illegal content be taken as a last resort only by an independent national judiciary and not by a commercial entity.
madam president, please show some mercy. please save us some hypocrisy. please spare us some crocodile tears on the freedom of speech. to most europeans with a little common sense, one thing is crystal clear: this house does not care about free speech. we did not see a reaction from the european parliament when tech giants removed president trump from their networks in january. we did not see a reaction when parler was removed from all major app stores. we did not see a reaction when vox activists were brutally attacked in barcelona last weekend. yes, there is pure censorship now in europe: in spain, against the only right-wing, traditional, national, conservative political party – vox. this is provided by twitter, for example. we didn’t see a reaction when this house promoted fake news about so-called ‘lgbti—free zones’ in poland. on the contrary, this european parliament promoted such false information. the fact of the matter is that this house does not permit opposing views which attack the liberal consensus. the new politburo will not tolerate diverging opinions which don’t accept the agenda of the day. the liberal establishment does not accept views against illegal migration, against ngos helping human trafficking in the mediterranean sea, against the lgbti ideology and propaganda, or against federalism. the ussr had a one-party system. this european parliament has a multi-party system, where political parties don’t really matter. nothing. the majority of this house takes the same stance on the issues i mentioned. but do not worry, please. freedom will always find a way, and liberty will always win. you cannot shut us down and we will not be silenced.
madam president, online media platforms have become some of the most powerful private social actors in history. the situation is unprecedented. in the past, their lack of action has repeatedly led to real world violence and recently they’ve suddenly silenced a democratically—elected president. the bottom line is that the freedom of speech of democratically—elected representatives, just like the uncontrolled spread of manipulative disinformation, cannot be just a private matter for global companies. decisions on these issues need democratic scrutiny and regulatory oversight. it is therefore one of the key regulatory challenges of our era to draft a new set of public rules to respond to the power and potential dangers of social media. we need more ambition than is currently envisaged in the recently proposed digital services act, especially when it comes to the responsibility and transparency of online platforms in the fight against hoaxes. the dsa is a much—needed step in the right direction, but we must do more to slow down the pervasive spread of manipulative and harmful content online. we will never be able to put an end to all deceiving lies, but we are surely able to give truth, facts, credible science and democracy a fighting chance in a digital space. i am convinced that europe and this house must lead these efforts on behalf of all european citizens, who deserve equal protection online across all member states.
madam president, the spread of hoaxes during the coronavirus crisis has had real consequences for the health and lives of people across europe. many people have believed the lies on social media that covid-19 is just a flu. sadly, many of them died. the coronavirus crisis has shown how weak we are in the fight against lies and disinformation, as well as in the fight against hatred, extremism, aggression, slander and attacks. a few weeks ago, we saw the former president of the united states mobilize the fanatics through social media. it's a good thing they finally froze his account. while many of us agree with this, we cannot leave such decisions in the hands of media owners whose primary goal is to make money. let's face it, the content of social media is completely out of our control. although it doesn't sound good, i'm convinced that lies and hoax about covid-19 have caused many deaths in europe. i am in no way in favor of regulating the freedom of social media. but we must prevent the spread of lies, hatred, extremism and disinformation.
madam president, today's prospectus, which provides investors with information about the company they're about to invest in, is hundreds of pages long. it is complicated and costs the issuer approximately one million euros. i therefore support the introduction of a new, cheaper recovery prospectus, which was designed as a temporary simplification in response to covid. the investor protection is not significantly affected and, at a maximum of 30 pages, the commission estimates that it will save companies 50% of the costs. however, i regret that the final text did not improve the indicators for the commission's evaluation report on the prospectus for the coming year. the eu's share of the global market is declining, with european capital markets far from reaching their full potential. we will therefore need relevant data to give us a true picture of the situation in order to improve the regulation in the future.
madam president, ostrovets nuclear power plant didn’t pass the eu stress test, but it’s fully functioning as we are speaking. ostrovets lies about 20 kilometres from the lithuanian border. such proximity can also be found in other member states, such as nuclear power plants in my country, slovakia, which are located only some dozens of kilometres from hungary or austria – but they have been upgraded to the highest possible safety standards. in this case, deep concerns have been expressed in member states like poland or lithuania, which we should not only understand but support. even if i am not against electricity produced by nuclear plants, nuclear safety should be a key priority, both within and beyond external borders. even if we cannot forget about the risk related to the deepening of energy poverty, especially in the post—pandemic recovery, we can’t accept lowered safety nuclear standards. we need stronger and more effective cooperation with the current regulatory authority in belarus to ensure that belarusian operators do not only apply a selective application of standards. we have to call on the belarusian authorities to have more transparency and respect for international nuclear and environmental safety standards.
madam president, 30 years following gaining independence, kazakhstan’s society has been changing a lot. the last positive signal announced was its intention to abolish the death penalty for all crimes. however, the ongoing crackdown on local human rights groups and the persecution of the human rights activists has provoked serious concerns as to whether kazakhstan’s leadership is genuinely interested in improving its human rights record. according to human rights groups, coordinated actions and unlawful acts are limiting fundamental rights and freedoms. we also have to be very cautious that the context of the covid-19 pandemic is not used as a pretext for further escalation of the situation. such doubts are damaging kazakhstan’s international reputation and the eu needs to indicate this to kazakhstan’s authorities clearly, as we have a joint partnership agreement with this central asian country. the eu should act consistently to weather any ongoing and long-standing threats towards citizens’ fundamental rights, both within and beyond our external borders, and kazakhstan cannot be an exception.
madam president, a vaccine is certainly the most effective means at this time to bring the coronavirus pandemic and its variants under real control and to allow our society to reopen, and especially to prevent hundreds of thousands more deaths. we all agree that in addition to vaccination and finding an effective treatment, the only possible means to protect yourself from the coronavirus is social distancing, disinfection, and especially wearing effective protective face masks, the best of which are respirators. other protective masks, especially clear masks, have not been authorised in all countries so far. but this has led to another discrimination, almost exclusion, that we've seen happening, of hearing-impaired people, who are also falling into severe depression from lack of communication. because these people need to be seen to read lips, wearing a clear mask is very important. we must therefore do everything we can at european union level to eliminate this discrimination as soon as possible and to enable these people, who are already affected by the corona pandemic, to return to normal life. the european commission must support the development, and also the production and, ultimately, the fair distribution of these transparent masks in all the countries of the union. of course, these masks have to provide adequate protection against the virus not only for the people who wear them on their faces, but also for the people they're addressing, and so the homologation is a real, i would say, protective and safeguarding element so that all these people can be protected from this nasty virus.
mr. speaker, today we commemorate the struggle of women for justice and equality. this tool is also about justice, carbon justice. we need to manage the climate crisis and take care of our own survival. we need to radically reduce greenhouse gas emissions, but not all countries are doing enough. we needed a carbon justice tool yesterday. the current system of free allocation of emissions needs to be fundamentally changed, as the european court of auditors has also stated. but some big polluters would like to have it all, from free emissions to a carbon tax on imports. but that would not be fair to our trading partners, and this tool is supposed to be fair to companies, people and the planet. at the same time, they must be fair to developing countries, which are the most affected by the climate crisis. the proceeds of this instrument must not be kept for ourselves. we certainly need them to finance climate action, to finance renewal as our own resources, but we also need to use them to address the climate crisis in developing countries. because like women, the climate needs equality and justice.
madam president, please accept my five signals on this subject. first, let us not confuse tax cooperation with tax harmonisation. secondly, tax competition is good, it is positive for the competitiveness of the european union and for economic growth. third, tax evasion must be curbed. since they distort fair competition between firms in the single market. fourth, member states should improve the quality and accuracy of the tax information they exchange with each other to make it more practical for other member states to use. without sufficient data quality, their use for reducing tax evasion will not be effective either. and fifth, this is precisely where the commission should be more proactive in the coordination process of information exchange.
madam president, today the european parliament will decide whether to continue the investment support programme known as the juncker fund. the efficiency margins and insufficient transparency of the juncker fund have been criticised by the european court of auditors in the past. i am afraid that even now we are not getting much more out of every euro invested by the citizens. the usefulness of the fund will be assessed, for example, by the number of undertakings supported. it's like when you invest in a highway, you don't measure the results by the number of miles built, but by the number of companies that got the money. i therefore believe that much work still needs to be done to increase efficiency and transparency. clearly, the money helps, but it needs to be delivered efficiently and transparently.
in writing. covid-19 has revealed the eu's unpreparedness and inability to respond to this type of health pandemic. health must be the absolute priority, and i am therefore pleased that it was the pato rlament that gave the decisive impetus to create a more functional, effective and, above all, resilient health programme. the new program will unfortunately have to settle for a much lower budget than many of us would like. when you think of the vastly greater sums we spend in the member states on armaments. but even with our pressure, the eu budget will go to health ten times more than it has been so far, which is a good start and will allow us to be better prepared for the health threats that are coming and to be more autonomous from outside players. the correct use of the programme should enable us to create the necessary stocks of medicines, vaccines, but also of medical protective equipment and, in particular, sufficient health workers, not only doctors but also nurses and nurses. i am pleased that a fifth of the budget will go to the prevention of diseases of civilization, such as cardiovascular diseases, which cause about half of all deaths in europe. money will also have to go to education and information to help people understand how their lifestyle can prevent serious diseases of civilization, not just tobacco but also, for example, sugar and unhealthy fats.

madam president! i'm not going to let you down. ladies and gentlemen! georgia is one of the most important partners of the european union. our relationship must be based on mutual trust and understanding. the crisis we have been witnessing for the past few months is a cause for concern because we are aware that building a lasting democratic system is a delicate process that requires the cooperation of all political parties and forces. it is very easy for one hasty decision to ruin what has been achieved so far. we don't want to criticize our friends or lecture them, but we can't pretend that nothing's going on either. many of the member states have had similar experiences and have been through similar crises. we are ready to share our knowledge and support democratic dialogue between the government and the opposition. georgians are unambiguously in favour of a modern and democratic state that is an integral part of the western world. the political class and party leaders in tbilisi should remember this and i think, after talking to them, they do. good luck with that.
madam president, i am deeply concerned with the ongoing situation in georgia and i would like to join the calls for the de—escalation of tensions. leaders across the political spectrum in georgia must find european solutions for the current crisis, solutions based on democratic standards, respect for dialogue between political opponents and the ability to compromise for the good of the georgian people. i appreciate the immediate european reaction to mediate in the current crisis and, while i welcome the efforts by president michel and the incoming personal envoy, christian danielson, i would like to emphasise that the european parliament can also play a constructive role in the mediation process. we, as members of this house, have parliamentary instruments, such as the jean monnet dialogue, and should stand ready to do our utmost to help georgia out of this crisis, not only by debating in brussels, but also on the ground in tbilisi. the bottom line is that pro—european and democratic georgia must prevail and we must also do everything in order to stop russia from using this crisis to meddle in the geopolitics of the southern caucasus.
madam president, the european semester is an important mechanism for coordinating the budgetary and economic policies of the member states. the general escape clause of the stability and growth pact has been activated for one year and according to the commission will be active at least until the end of next year. some interpret this decision as a failure of fiscal rules to adapt to the reality of new high debts. but the rules did not fail, in my view, it was the long-term failure to enforce and enforce the rules that failed. member states that did their homework in good times, made structural reforms and consolidated public finances now have reserves to help their economies. without reforms and without sufficient consolidation, member states also lacked the fiscal reserves linked to the european rescue packages. if the reserves of the contending states were distributed equally among all the states, it could happen that in good times they would stop making them - the contending states - and in the next crisis there would simply be nothing to redistribute. let's remember that socialism ended because there was no one left to take.
in writing. – we are happy that our commissioner launched the action plan, and we welcome the principles that stand for it. our family has long stood and pushed for very ambitious yet necessary measures regarding for example the situation of the mobile or cross-border workers in eu. the pandemic unfortunately shows how vulnerable and precarious these workers can be. let me remind you the case of romanian workers sent in unsafe ways by the liberal government last summer to do seasonal work in germany. we need to ensure a minimum rights directive for these workers, and to ensure that they know about their rights. people have often very little knowledge of their rights. i strongly believe that we should propose an information instrument at their departure so that they can easily understand why they should not accept precarious working conditions. we cannot go below our agreed ambitions on the just transition. we need to ensure that the social pillar is in line with this change of society and that there are enough actions to implement it properly so that nobody is left behind. the member states should provide for an actual plan for the workers and the commission should assist on that.
in writing. if there were any doubts about the ability of a good presidency of the council of the european union to make a difference, the presentation of the proposal for the action plan for the implementation of the european pillar of social rights dispels them in an exemplary way. the formulation of this plan was accelerated by the portuguese presidency's convening of a social summit in porto in may. i would also like to stress the involvement of the european commission in this process, which is in line with a central political demand of the european parliament, and in particular of the socialist and democratic group. the action plan is a starting point for a debate which will lead to its adoption at the social summit, reinforced by a joint declaration by the commission, the member states' governments and the european parliament, reinforcing the commitment to its objectives. it is essential that the plan gives a strong impetus to the creation and protection of fair and decent employment, the qualification of people and poverty reduction, inspiring the strengthening of the social and inclusive dimension of the european union's recovery, resilience and modernisation process, in conjunction with the implementation of other strategic priorities such as the green and digital transition.

because it works it must be defended: the european union is committed to protecting the common good and ensuring the participation, transparency and involvement of citizens in democratic life, and to fostering the participation of our society. it is urgent to renew our democratic pact, renewing the link with our society. a genuine european public space is emerging, as we have seen more than ever in the adversities of this year: the crisis has revealed a genuine europeanisation of public opinion, the conviction of our unity and the awareness of a common destiny are now shared by the majority of european citizens and decision-makers, and they feed off each other. we must now lay the foundations for a new european social contract and strengthen europe so that it no longer suffers the emergence of future crises, but is equipped with the strategic tools and capacity to be more resilient. "listen, discuss and change europe together": the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. but above all, we are firmly committed today to ensuring that the recommendations and conclusions of the conference are followed up: it is essential that this exercise should lead to concrete action, legislative changes, treaty changes, if this is desired and desired. we are committed to breaking down taboos and to ensuring that the results can offer a real vision of our european project. our credibility as representatives of the institutions, as representatives of the citizens, is at stake. our future and the future of our democracy is at stake. we now have the opportunity to rediscover the soul of the european project and make it live in the present. we therefore invite all european citizens to take part in the conference and to build the europe of tomorrow so that it can truly become 'their europe'.
acting president of the council. mr president of the european parliament, madam president of the european commission, ladies and gentlemen, when all over europe we are facing a pandemic that has generated the greatest economic and social crisis since the second world war, when in our hospitals health professionals are struggling to save lives, when we are in a race against the clock for vaccination, when millions of workers are losing their jobs and thousands of businesses are facing the risk of bankruptcy, when uncertainty, anxiety and fear mark our collective present, the convening of this conference on the future of europe is a message of confidence and hope for the future that we are sending to all europeans. the confidence that we will beat the pandemic and get through the crisis. the hope that together we will build a future europe, one that is fair, green and digital. we are therefore glad that we were able to overcome the impasse which had paralysed us. and i want to thank everyone here in the european parliament, in the commission, in the council, who has worked to make this agreement possible. we know that we do not all have the same vision of the future europe or of the future of europe, but that is precisely why the conference on the future of europe is a decisive moment for us to be able to discuss, without taboos but with a straight face, the diversity of our visions. only then can we overcome our divisions and recover what unites us and strengthen what unites us, as has always happened over the almost 64 years since the signing of the treaty of rome. and the time for that discussion is now, because we need to prepare now so that when we beat the pandemic, we're ready and we don't waste a second building the future. the union needs to be strengthened by the strength of its citizens. so this cannot be a conference of institutions about institutions. it must be a conference of european citizens on what they want and how they want the future of our union. a conference on the political issues that really concern europeans. in addition to the variations in the economic situation - health today, of course, public debt ten years ago - the eurobarometer gives us a very clear picture of the issues that continue to worry our fellow citizens the most and that require our response: economic issues and unemployment, in particular climate change, migration, terrorism. we have a strategic agenda for the future, uniting the european institutions around a common vision, a europe stronger in protecting citizens and freedoms, with a dynamic, green, fair and social economic base, and able to promote european interests and values on the world stage. but we need europeans to embrace this design and feel it as their own, because they are the final recipients. that's why this conference is so important. because it will allow us to bring europeans into the public sphere, and it is important that european citizens can have a space where they can discuss their aspirations and expectations with their representatives, so that public policies can provide concrete answers to the citizens' everyday needs. so that no citizen feels left behind. we can't waste any more time. it's time to deliver. it's time to start building our future together. (applause from the left)
president of the commission. – mr president, we all have our own dreams when we think about the future of europe. for me, this would be a europe that is leading the world in a green and digital transition. a europe that embraces the full creativity and diversity of all of its citizens and leaves no one behind. a europe that is able to fight recurrent pandemics and protect its citizens. a europe whose democracies are resilient against fake news and the distorting effects of social media. a europe that is a strong voice for freedom and reason in a world where autocrats challenge our way of life. this, and much more, is my dream for europe. one out of 450 million dreams. today we want to hear about the europe our citizens are dreaming of. we want people to take the lead on issues that matter to them and affect their everyday life. today we signed our joint declaration on the conference on the future of europe. some may ask, ‘why now, in the middle of a pandemic, a crisis?’ my answer is that it is exactly in a time of crisis that we see where europe works for people and where we have to get better. the past months have shown that europe can achieve if it has the competences and the necessary means to act. take next generation eu and our european budget: eur 1.8 trillion to build forward better. an unprecedented investment to come out of this crisis together: more sustainable, more digital, fairer and more resilient. stronger than ever. but during this pandemic we have also seen difficulties. where europe’s competences are weak. only gradually did solidarity and support overcome closed borders. it took too much time until there was a consensus on how to better prepare for the next round in the fight against the virus and its future variants. until everyone understood that going it alone was not an option. let me be very clear: this conference on the future of europe is not just another conference for what some call the ‘brussels bubble’. this conference has to go beyond brussels, has to go beyond national capitals, because for this conference to succeed, we want to reach what some call the ‘silent majority’. we want to hear from european citizens in their full diversity: young and old, city dwellers and rural residents. from erasmus students to those who took to the streets in the pulse of europe demonstrations. as well as from those who have their doubts about whether creating an ever—closer union is the right road to take. we want to hear from all! because it is as true today as it was in the days of robert schuman, one of our founding fathers, that when it comes to the future of europe, no effort is too much. no dream is too bold.
the debate on the council and commission statements on the government attempts to silence the free media in poland, hungary and slovenia (2021/2560 ((rsp)) continues. i would remind members that in all the debates of this part-session there will be no catch-the-eye procedures or blue-card questions, as has been the custom. in addition, as in previous sessions, distance speeches are planned from our liaison offices in the member states.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, free and pluralistic media are, and must remain, a central pillar of our european democratic societies, founded on the promotion and protection of the rule of law. the freedom and pluralism of the media also reflects the underlying principle of our freedom of expression and information, as enshrined in the eu charter of fundamental rights. in this context, the eu and national institutions must ensure that independent, free and diverse media thrive, that journalists are protected from any harmful interference in their functions, and that citizens have access to reliable information. this should be true for every single member state. the covid-19 pandemic has exacerbated challenges that were already present before for media and journalists, such as the sharp decline in revenues and the numerous transformations brought about by the new digital environment. yet a free, independent and pluralistic media has never been as important as today to ensure a constructive, rational and open debate in our democracies. we have all seen the damage that conspiracy theories, fake news and disinformation can generate in our societies. they are the breeding ground for distrust and polarisation. in current times, journalists are not just watchdogs for democracy. they are key workers in the protection of all of us from the virus of disinformation. the variety of information and the plurality of voices are part and parcel of our democratic systems. a trustworthy media system is key to ensuring that all citizens are able to participate in the democratic debate through informed decision-making. the european democracy action plan, the eu media and audiovisual action plan and the digital services act, recently tabled by the commission, are important steps forward and set out actions to strengthen europe’s media ecosystem. on 23 february, i chaired a meeting of european affairs ministers, where we discussed the democracy action plan. we considered it a timely initiative, aiming to strengthen democratic processes and address currently existing vulnerabilities, including those threatening free and pluralistic media and freedom of speech. back in november, two days after the parliament adopted its resolution on strengthening media freedom, on 25 november, the council sent a strong message on the importance of safeguarding a free and pluralistic media system and also in its council conclusions. ministers will continue to highlight the importance of media freedom, including through the framework of the european rule of law mechanism. indeed, media pluralism and media freedom constitutes one of the four pillars of the rule of law annual report prepared by the commission. the presidency is committed to pursuing the annual council rule of law dialogue, initiated by the german presidency during their presidency, and to continue discussing these issues. on 20 april, we will have a country—specific discussion with ministers of european affairs concerning five member states – germany, ireland, greece, spain and france – and soon all member states will follow by protocol order. last year, the discussions demonstrated the willingness of member states to cooperate and learn from each other, including on media freedom. we trust that every member state will fully contribute to this important debate and ensure that media pluralism and freedom remain a reality. we all have a joint responsibility to guarantee that the rule of law and fundamental rights remain the cornerstone of our common european project.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, thank you for including this important issue on the plenary agenda for today. media are not just an economic sector, but also an important pillar of democracy and the rule of law. press freedom and media pluralism are vital to democracy. independent media play an essential role to help citizens make informed decisions, to fight disinformation and the manipulation of the democratic debate. that is why the commission has included media freedom and pluralism as one pillar of our annual rule of law report, which was the first one published last september. the rule of law report assessed, in particular, the independence of the media regulatory authorities, transparency of media ownership, state advertising, the safety of journalists, and access to information. it looked at the situation in all member states, including hungary, poland and slovenia. the problems, and our concerns, were made very clear. we will also continue to cover media pluralism and freedom in this year’s rule of law report, which is expected in july. each rule of law report is preceded by fact-finding visits in all eu countries and discussions with national authorities and a wide range of stakeholders. the media are also covered in the article 7 procedure launched by this house in relation to hungary. i expect us to soon have the occasion to come back to the article 7 procedure in the general affairs council, where we have to discuss recent developments in relation to the media. the covid-19 pandemic has shown, more than ever, the essential role of journalists to inform citizens, but, at the same time, the media are facing an economic crisis which started well before the pandemic. more than ever, it is time to support the work of the media, and not to put additional burdens or pressures on them. this is exactly what the commission proposed in december, when, for the first time ever, we presented a comprehensive approach for the media. with the european democracy action plan, we put the media where they should be: at the core of democracy. let me highlight two initiatives, in particular, that we will present this year. a recommendation to member states to improve the safety of journalists, and an initiative on tackling abusive litigation, the so-called slapp cases, (strategic lawsuits against public participation). very often, threats and groundless lawsuits are used to silence free media. we will also take action to increase the transparency of media ownership and of political advertising. with the media and audiovisual action plan, which we also adopted in december, we helped the media recover economically from the crisis, adapt to the digital transformation and develop new business models. the stronger the economic situation of the media, the easier it is to resist political pressure, but there should be no political pressure in the first place. strong leaders are those that gain respect through their actions, that accept diversity of opinions and that allow citizens to be duly informed, not trolls that try to silence critical voices. in a democracy, independent media should do their work and ask questions without fear or favour. our job as politicians is to answer with facts, not with attacks. unfortunately, in recent months, additional worrying developments have happened. allow me to name some of them, which are just examples of the worrying trends. in hungary, most notably, we have seen that the independent klubradio was denied the renewal of its licence on grounds which have raised concerns, and it remains off air awaiting the outcome of a new procedure. in poland, draft legislation on an advertising tax targeting media outlets led to black screens as a sign of protest. i spoke about it here in parliament after this happened. this draft is now being reviewed, and we expect that the voices that raised concerns will be properly heard. in slovenia, continuous attempts to undermine the sustainable funding and the independence of the national press agency and frequent verbal attacks against journalists are also a cause of serious concern. for each of these cases, the commission has been in contact with the national authorities and continues to monitor the situation. let me assure you that the commission does not hesitate to act when there are issues of compliance of national laws or decisions with european union rules. in particular, i would like to highlight that we are analysing closely the transposition of the revised audiovisual media services directive, which introduced, for the first time, new independence requirements for national media regulators. and we will also watch closely the transposition of the modernised copyright framework and continue striving for a level playing field in the online world. this is also the aim of the new digital services and digital markets acts. this is essential for the sustainability of the media sector, as i mentioned earlier. in the concrete cases at hand, we watch, for instance, the potential impact on telecom and taxation rules. however, the reality is, as you know well, that the competences of the commission when it comes to media are very limited. whilst we will use those competences in a very diligent manner, i want us to identify how we can widen and strengthen the toolbox that the commission has, from financial support to regulation and enforcement actions. we need a tool which recognises the role of media as the key players in democratic society. at this moment, we only have the rules which recognise the role of the media as actors on the european single market, and this is what is limiting our ability to act. we will play our role, but governments also need to fulfil their obligations to ensure that media freedom is safeguarded and to enable a healthy environment for media pluralism.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, democracy dies in darkness, and dark it is in poland today. dark like the blacked—out websites and front pages of polish media protesting against the plan to strip independent media of their revenues and to silence critical voices. because we have been witnessing for years now a continuous attempt by the law and justice government in poland to silence free media, to deprive polish citizens of their right to information and to undermine the rule of law. from the takeover of public channels and turning them into propaganda channels, from restricting the access of certain journalists to the parliament, to pouring millions of polish zloty into those media that are faithful to the government and also into public television that has been turned into non–stop propaganda, spewing out hate speech and xenophobia, with the most dramatic result being the murder of paweł adamowicz, the mayor of gdańsk. we shall never, ever forget this. the rule of law, independence of the judiciary and media freedom must be upheld. an attack on polish independent media is an attack on us all. it is an attack on european values. the situation was best summarised yesterday by adam michnik, editor–in–chief of the independent daily gazeta wyborcza. he said ‘we are treated as enemies for the simple reason that we are independent and we have the courage to speak truth to power’. we stand with those who have the courage to speak truth to power. we stand with polish journalists and free media everywhere, and we call on everybody, including the council and the commission, to do the same.
the commission is therefore proposing to amend the proposal to the effect that the commission should be able to take part in the discussions. the commission is not black days for free media in europe. in poland, several media outlets turned on black ads on february 10 in protest against a new advertising tax. officially, the tax targets internet giants like google, but in reality, the pis wants to silence media outlets that are critical of the government. in december, it had already acquired indirect access to two dozen regional media outlets through the purchase of the polska press newspaper group by a state-controlled oil company. on february 14, klubrádió was turned on analog for the last time, because the licenses were not renewed. since then, the last nationally known independent radio station in hungary is only available online, reaching only a fraction of its previous audience. in slovenia, there have been repeated verbal attacks by prime minister janša against a journalist and at the same time the situation around the slovenian press agency is getting worse. the government had suspended funding again. freedom of the media and media diversity are essential pillars of democracy. but it is not enough to analyse their situation in annual reports, to emphasize their importance in fine words and to give exhortations. we need to see the consequences and the council must act.
on behalf of the renew group. – mr president, i say to madam vice-president and to my colleagues: thanks for being here and for this debate. i think everybody in this room would agree that media freedom and pluralism are key for democracies not just to thrive, but to survive. at this moment, in many member states, the red lights are indeed flashing. it has been said here, of course: in slovenia, you see the prime minister busy with personal attacks on journalists, not just local, but also brussels journalists, and launching an attack against the press agency. in slovakia, actually my country, this was also the case before the murder of ján kuciak, so i remember it very well. poland was also mentioned, with its new tax law, and it just so happens that the tax law targets those media outlets which dare to be critical of the government. and then, obviously, you have hungary, where the media landscape has been almost completely ruined over the past few years. and just the other week, one of the last independent radio stations, klubradio, was forced off the air. i have to say that the response of the eu has so far been inadequate. there is no condemnation from other member states. the commission has taken little concrete action, and this has to change. i welcome the fact that the vice–president has suggested some new tools. the parliament would support the commission in that, but it is time to act now. otherwise, it could very well be too late for these democracies because the attack on free media is often the beginning of the creep of authoritarianism.
mr president, do you really want to condemn poland, hungary and slovenia for wanting to impose rules on the mass media? for the mass media to pay some taxes? for the mass media to no longer be able to write whatever they want, whether it's intimidation, fear, or often manipulation of public opinion, which they unfortunately often abuse. what's the problem, that the far-left media will no longer control 95 percent of the media share, but only 85 percent? you know, it's very interesting that the european union is silent when the mainstream media is literally censoring conservative politicians, when they're lynching anti-immigration activists, when they're lynching defenders of traditional values. but when someone dares to criticize the media or ngos, suddenly there is a big shake-up in brussels. this is unbelievable.
mr president, madam commissioner, colleagues, words are not harmless. verbal attacks on journalists can actually even lead to murder. as we have seen in malta and slovakia, where the murders were preceded by endless verbal attacks by political leaders. i’m very worried to see now that, in the slovenian government, they are taking on the same habits of attacking journalists. that not only has a chilling effect on the freedom of the media and on their freedom of expression, but it actually gives people almost literally a licence to kill it. it adds to a climate of hatred, and we should speak out. i hope that in slovenia, but also in other countries where this is taking place, we can make sure that it doesn’t run off track the way it has in hungary and poland, where we can say that the media are no longer free and therefore they are no longer complete democracies. and finally, madam commissioner, i would like to ask you to put forward legislative proposals not just on slapp, but also on transparency of media ownership, because we see how governments are using their power to buy the media, to control the media. so please, legislative proposals. mr president, a free and independent media is the backbone of any flourishing democracy. if we stood here some 10 years ago, we might not be having this discussion at all, but we do have a european problem with the media situation. let’s face the facts. the media situation in a number of central european countries has worsened in recent years. the world press freedom index has seen hungary, poland, even slovenia, but also the czech republic, my own country slovakia, and bulgaria drop significantly from the better positions they held just a couple of years ago. the political atmosphere and public environment in which journalists work matter. in slovakia and malta, we learned the hard way. we witnessed the very extreme: the death of journalists. i’m always sad to watch heightened tensions online and offline, and i’m very concerned when politicians use social media to attack journalists. to reverse this trend, each of us, dear colleagues, can make a difference by using words responsibly. we must also ask the commission, madam commissioner, for a more thorough and regular monitoring of the media situation and media ownership across all member states, and for stronger european support for independent media. journalists deserve better national and european protection through anti-slapp laws, punishment of crimes against them, and strong institutional foundations for independent public broadcasters. the bottom line is that european democracy will only endure with media that work in line with europe’s values. there is no other way.
for the ecr group. madam president, as a member of the european parliament from slovakia, i have my reasons for welcoming the experts from the european medicines agency at the beginning of my speech and thanking them for their independence in assessing individual vaccines. now, to the point. national recovery plans go beyond the electoral period and, in particular, those countries that lack continuity in their political culture and that start each electoral period from scratch, must achieve an absolute broad social consensus in the plans, including the social partners and including broad civil society. the final payment of the huge money package will reach the member states on 31 december 2026. time is short, money is plentiful, and those member states in particular who have been unable to draw on european resources for a long time should wake up, because of excessive bureaucracy and because they look at every applicant as a potential thief, because they have not been able to deal systematically with corruption in the euro funds. as chair of the employment committee, i will ensure that investments and reforms contribute to a fair recovery from the crisis and that they reach those most affected by the crisis.
in writing. the introduction of so-called green covid passports must not discriminate against those who, for various reasons, could not be vaccinated, and must only be a temporary measure that will be abolished after the end of the pandemic. of course, in the event of further possible global health crises, it will be possible to consider renewing them. it is a way of making it easier for europeans, including slovaks, to travel this summer and of unifying the countries' different approaches to the treatment of incoming tourists and other travellers. of course, it is still necessary to ensure that vaccination is not mandatory and therefore the introduction of a covid passport must also allow people to choose to prove themselves with alternative confirmations to vaccination, namely a negative pcr test or a certificate of covid-19 disease recovery. the decision on which vaccines outside the ema's official portfolio will be recognised by individual countries must remain entirely within their competence. i appreciate that the european commission allows member states to include vaccines outside the ema portfolio in the green passport, in addition to vaccines registered by the ema. i believe that many eu countries will soon accept the russian sputnik v vaccine, which is still being evaluated by the ema and has already been purchased by some eu countries. in order for our citizens to really benefit from these green covid passports, it is necessary that they enter into force before the summer holidays, under the conditions mentioned.
in writing. the european commission's digital agenda 2030 vision for a digital europe that puts the digital development of businesses and citizens at its core. support the objective that by 2030 three quarters of enterprises should use cloud computing services, big data and artificial intelligence technologies. it is also important for competitiveness that 90% of smes should reach basic digital maturity. however, in order to achieve this, the commission should provide adequate support to european businesses, in particular the sme sector. the commission's vision is also in line with the objectives of the hungarian government's digital welfare programme. the digital well-being capital programme, introduced in 2019, supports projects for the development of digital products and services by domestic micro, small and medium-sized enterprises and startups with capital investments of at least huf 10 million and up to huf 500 million. by the end of last year, the government had distributed a total of some huf 3.7 billion in aid to enterprises. it is important that the commission proactively explores the potential of digital technology, data and artificial intelligence to increase the productivity of enterprises and the well-being of the workforce. the hungarian government's artificial intelligence strategy also stands out, which envisages that by 2030 one million citizens will find work with more significant added value from a digital point of view, with the help of artificial intelligence, hungary's gdp will grow, and the productivity of smes will increase by 26%.
in writing. – data is an essential resource necessary for training artificial intelligence. furthermore, the processing of anonymized medical data will enable us to draw very useful conclusions regarding the spread of pandemics, such as covid-19 and its various mutations. it will also allow us to make progress in figuring out treatments for rare and chronic illnesses. in order to be able to achieve these scientific developments and to be able to find potential efficiency gains in our everyday lives, for instance in the field of energy efficiency, data needs to be shared and it needs to be accessible for those that need to process it. although some fundamental safeguards are necessary, we must make sure that the regulatory burden and cost of compliance does not overwhelm the administrative capabilities of our smes. it is precisely our smes that are the cradle of innovation in europe. in addition to fostering innovation, they provide a significant number of jobs, forming the backbone of our economy. therefore, our data strategy and its implementation are of key importance for our future prosperity. we need to get it right from the beginning!
the own-initiative report under discussion puts the european parliament in a position to develop its role as co-legislator more effectively and with greater influence in the legislative package for the second wave of digitalisation and the digital transition, and in particular in the regulations on data management, digital services and digital markets. i would stress the broad commitment that has been made, giving force to a vision of the data economy that is closely linked to the objectives of the european green deal, respecting the principles of transparency, trust, the centrality of people, human rights and democracy. an important commitment also in the priority given to creating more services and opportunities for citizens and for businesses, in particular for small and medium-sized enterprises. the content of the report is in line with the view i have put forward in several ongoing debates and negotiations that a european citizenship pact for data is needed, which allows citizens' data to be converted into services of general interest and easy access, promoting democratic, green and inclusive digitisation. the focus on skills and interoperability is key to this approach.
rapporteur. – madam president, the progress report that we’ll be discussing and voting on today covers a period of two years. this should have been full of dynamic events for the western balkans. despite some positive developments like the enlargement methodology, the decision of the european council on north macedonia and on albania at the end of last year was harmful for the western balkans and had a direct negative impact on the respective countries and their societies. but the covid pandemic that hit the region dramatically has shown us something else. europe has managed to prove that it stands behind its partners. the mobilisation of eur 3.3 billion for the countries of the region to deal with the effects of the pandemic was a clear demonstration of the european solidarity which has long been called into question. it has now proven to be real and this is well underlined in all four reports, because the eu’s engagement with the western balkans surpasses that of any other region and is in our strategic interest. in particular, on the republic of north macedonia, as standing rapporteur i would work to highlight some important elements of the report. but before that, let me first express my gratitude for the constructive work of the shadow rapporteurs, whose contributions undoubtedly enriched the report. north macedonia has come along and uneasy way to building reforms and compromises, until it reached the stage of starting formal eu accession negotiations and deservedly became the 30th nato member. the report clearly reflects that. it is an objective account of what we have seen in that country in recent years. indeed, consistent progress was achieved. the country demonstrated continuity in its dedicated commitment to the european path and understanding of the need to implement stable and inclusive democratic reforms, to stand for democracy and the rule of law in order to reinforce the climate of mutual trust. the report recognises that and further calls on all political parties to remain constructive, to refrain from using nationalistic and inflammatory rhetoric. engaging in a constructive dialogue is a prerequisite of good governance in the pursuit of the cross—party goal of accession. maintaining a steady pace in adopting eu reforms as a means of ensuring efficient functioning of democratic institutions and procedures is also well recognised. of course, some setbacks such as the impact of the covid—19 pandemic and the tensions in the diplomatic relations with bulgaria were noted as well. but north macedonia shouldn’t stop with the reforms as in some areas, like the fight against corruption, organised crime and media freedom, more reforms are needed. reforms are needed in areas like more inclusivity in the society. we need to see deeper and faster reforms, and we need to see progress for sure. it is further important that public administration reforms are prioritised by systematically applying merit—based standards in public appointments and in promotions. the cooperative approach of north macedonia throughout the negotiations of the prespa agreement with greece and the treaty on good neighbourly relations with bulgaria is also underlined. having said that i hope that both countries – north macedonia and bulgaria – will resume the constructive dialogue on the pending bilateral issues in order to achieve sustainable results. reaching a compromise over an action plan of concrete measures, implementation of which is to be regularly assessed in accordance with the friendship treaty, would start the process and would allow the council to convene the first intergovernmental conference as soon as possible. i am convinced that further delays will put at risk the credibility, objectivity and reliability of the accession process. we have to focus on the bigger picture. through building trust together we are building our common future within the eu as we need bridges that unite us more than walls that divide. няколко думи и на български. когато едно доверие е нарушено, безспорно то трябва да бъде възстановено и най-добрият начин за това е да се върнем към основите на договора. само той ще ни даде възможност да начертаем пътната карта на региона, който неминуемо е свързан с интеграцията на всички страни в европейския съюз. от това ще спечели регионалното сътрудничество, от това ще спечелят добросъседските отношения, от това ще спечели и целият европейски съюз. finally, on the kosovo report, for which i am shadow rapporteur, the country has
