for the ecr group. mr president, the objective of the corporate tax reform should be to change the rules for the digital age, while ensuring that the benefits of tax competition are preserved. it is not true that corporate taxation is in crisis in 27 countries of the european union. it's only in crisis in some states. in slovakia, for example, the collection of this tax increased by 63 percent in nominal terms between 2011 and 2019, and gross domestic product increased by 36 percent in nominal terms during the same period. it is precisely tax competition that makes it possible to keep the tax system functioning, since it exposes its greatest shortcomings. the commission should therefore share best practices with the countries with the most advanced rules in the countries with the most problems.
madam president, it's important that people around the world are vaccinated as soon as possible. until a critical number of people are vaccinated, no one is vaccinated. this is a clear space for solidarity. but it should not be done by very rich countries taking away from private companies their hard-earned assets, like patents, but by buying vaccines from manufacturers and passing them cheaply, or for free, to very poor people in poor countries. if we decide to expropriate the patents without compensation, the vaccine for the next mutation of the virus may not be developed. if we want to increase the supply of vaccines, we have to be willing to pay more for them. a higher price for vaccines will mean a higher production. not an hour, not a week, but almost certainly a month, and for concrete, a few months. last but not least, we should not send such a negative signal about business and the market economy. the development of a coronavirus vaccine in less than a year is a great achievement, especially for the private sector, which few people believed in a year ago.
mr. president, protecting the taxpayers' money of the european union from being misused by the oligarchs must be our priority. however, existing mechanisms are clearly inadequate. control at national level is weak, at european level it takes an unreasonable amount of time. this is the state of affairs for those who use public money to expand their empires. this is also for the assistance of the national authorities. the problem is all the more pronounced when the highest political representation of an eu member state is in conflict of interest, and i therefore welcome the conclusion of the european commission's audit of 23 april this year, which clearly confirmed the conflict of interest of the prime minister of the czech republic and proposed a remedy to the situation. where enforcement of the law is weak, the system allows for the misuse of public funds. this concerns all levels of the public sphere, including important policies, agricultural and cohesion. we need to put in place an effective control system that is resistant to abuse by the political elite. we need to establish the same legal definition and uniform interpretation of conflict of interest at eu and member state level. at present, no eu legal standard obliges member states to publish the names of the final beneficiaries of grants. game theory is relentless in this. sooner or later, in any flock of pigeons, there will be a hawk who will exploit such rules to his advantage. a system that truly protects public finances will not tolerate such abuse.
mr president, commissioner, we all agree that taxpayers' money throughout the union must be used for its intended purpose and the european parliament is involved in protecting it. unfortunately, the way the world is, there's always been a drive to circumvent the rules and appropriate money that's not yours, and it doesn't escape even the biggest businessmen and top politicians. the case of czech prime minister andrej babiš is becoming such a case study. the european union has adopted several measures to combat the misuse of the european budget, firstly the financial regulation on conflicts of interest and then the regulation on conditionality of the use of the european budget. andrej babiš, as prime minister, has agreed to these measures. i'm sure you do. he certainly does not want his tax money, which is used by his companies that benefit from european funds, to be misused by another prime minister. however, when the commission identified his conflict of interest and invited him to suspend his business activities and thus end his conflict of interest, he did not do so. the european commission, even after the czech government's response, maintains its position on the conflict of interest of prime minister babiš. the european commission does not say that the czech republic has a conflict of interest. the conflict of interest is prime minister andrej babiš, who is holding our country hostage. in this context, i'm interested to know, commissioner, how you feel about the fact that the government is drafting the position paper for andrei babiš. is that an acceptable course of action? i would also like to support the idea that if we want to prevent misuse of funds in the future, we must channel the revenue from the common agricultural policy. it's a call to the council, please, for the council to respond.
mr president, the czech republic is not often among the countries we discuss here because of its particular problems. i am therefore very sorry that the government of andrej babiš is putting the czech republic in this position. i want to emphasize that we are not talking about the case of the evil brussels accusing our czech partners of something meaningless. on the contrary, we are talking about a very specific problem that can concern every european politician. there is a clear and direct conflict of interest here, the sole reason for which is the unfair business practices of the czech prime minister. politics in a democratic europe must not be the continuation of the business interests of selected individuals. this kind of behavior, this abuse of political power, we cannot tolerate in a society that is based on rules. a european partner and a responsible politician follows the rules and does not look for ways to circumvent them or to impose them on others. i thank from this place the many skilled czech officials, diplomats and everyone who does not shrink from protecting the interests of the czech republic and the european community. your work, ladies and gentlemen, for your country stands out particularly compared to the dishonest efforts of those who protect their positions of power and relationship with the sitting prime minister. i am very sorry that the specific conflict of interest of one politician has reached the stage where in the czech republic opponents are being abused, where my colleague tomáš zdechovský and his family are receiving unacceptable threats for their work. in europe, we have civilized ways of dealing with conflicts of interest. ladies and gentlemen, if prime minister babiš still feels that he is wrong, despite the credible opinion of experts, he can go to the european court of justice or he can simply resign from the role of politician.
madam president, only those who have experienced discrimination, attacks, stigmatisation, assault, simply because they have been labelled as different, inferior or inferior, can appreciate the european agency for fundamental rights the most. its work has become key to the development of effective policies to combat discrimination, racism, homophobia, xenophobia and inequalities at european as well as national level. fourteen years after the fra was established, we regret to say that the status of many individuals, groups, communities and minorities has deteriorated. it's easy to point the finger at a small ethnic group and say, "they're to blame". it is easy to blame other people for societal faults, injustices, or shortcomings. the covid-19 pandemic has shown and highlighted how fragile our society is, highlighting the gaps, inequalities, as well as the importance of protecting basic human rights. the results of the fra's high-quality analyses or research have become irreplaceable and still needed. i therefore fully support the european commission's proposal that the founding regulation of the european agency for human rights reflect current changes and bring them into line with the legal and institutional situation. the founding regulation should be reformatted in a clear manner, defining the agency's remit in such a way as to avoid any ambiguity, interpretation or limitation. i consider this to be key to the effective implementation of its mandate. in this context, i would like to call on the european council to adopt a decision to this effect as soon as possible.
member of the commission. – mr president, let me, first of all, thank the rapporteur mr doleschal as well as all the shadow rapporteurs for their work on this important file and for their spirit of compromise. i hope this spirit can also be maintained in the course of the upcoming interinstitutional negotiations. the aarhus convention links government accountability and environmental protection and thus goes to the heart of the relationship between people and governments. access to information, public participation in decision-making and access to justice in environmental matters are more important than ever. i’m therefore pleased to see the progress made by parliament on the commission’s proposal to revise the aarhus regulation. this week’s plenary vote is a key step in the legislative process. the timetable is very demanding and swift progress is essential. we need to ensure that a political agreement on the legislative amendment can be reached on time, and ideally before the summer break, to allow us to present the results of the meeting of the parties to the aarhus convention in october 2021. this should bring a longstanding discussion on the eu’s compliance with the aarhus convention to an end. the amendments tabled today by the committee on the environment, public health and food safety are very ambitious. while the key objective is to accommodate all four key recommendations made by the compliance committee in its advice of 12 february, some amendments, however, go beyond the committee’s advice and address elements unrelated to the case that triggered the review of the aarhus regulation. as usual, at this stage of the legislative procedure, the commission reserves its position to take into account the outcome of parliament’s plenary vote, as well as the upcoming discussions in the council. we will then define our position for the trilogue negotiations at a later stage, once we know where both co-legislators stand so that we can play our role as honest broker in the most effective way. however, i want to share some comments and concerns with you already as regards a number of amendments proposed in your report, and draw your attention to their far-reaching implications. with such an important and complex file, we need to clearly understand the exact implications of the changes we propose, as some of them may significantly impact the eu institutions and the functioning of the whole eu legal order. we all agree that our goal is to ensure compliance with the eu’s international obligations under the aarhus convention. better engagement with the public is also essential to the success of the european green deal. if we want to deliver lasting change, the public needs to remain a driving force in the transition. across the eu, environmental non-governmental organisations (ngos) play a vital advocacy role, and as commissioner, i witness very directly the key role that ngos play every day when it comes to monitoring the good implementation of eu environmental law. under the commission’s original proposals, ngos would have even broader possibilities to exercise their role as watchdogs. acts on the eu institutions would be opened up for review under many policy areas, including the environment, transport infrastructure and major energy projects. the expansion would bring sweeping changes to the current system and new opportunities to hold the eu institutions to account. the importance of the proposed changes cannot be emphasised too highly. taken together with the priority actions outlined in the commission communication on access to justice in environmental matters, we are confident that these proposed changes by the commission, as long as they respect the features of the eu legal order, would be the best way to comply fully and effectively with the requirements of the aarhus convention. at the meeting of the parties in october, we must stand united. we must be able to show the progress we have achieved, but it will also be important to be firm where specificities and fundamental principles of the eu legal order need to be taken into account, and where, simply put, the aarhus regulation is not the right tool to bring about further change. in the interest of effective access to justice and of a timely and successful conclusion of our legislative negotiations, i am convinced that it will be important to focus on issues that have been raised by the compliance committee in its findings and advice, to consider and respect the specificities and fundamental principles of the eu legal order, and to remain realistic. this means to consider the practical implications and the feasibility and effectiveness of our proposals. let’s be very clear: in a european union with a population of almost 450 million, the practical consequences of opening up the review process, free of charge to each and every individual, are far-reaching. there is a genuine risk that the system will be unable to cope and that effective case handling becomes impossible. there is also a risk of not being able to establish clear, fair and non-discriminatory criteria to distinguish between those who should and those who should not have access to a review procedure, and the proposed use of delegated acts in this regard raises at least certain institutional and legal questions. i hope we can all agree that the eu institutions and the court of justice should not spend taxpayers’ money on dealing with huge numbers of inadmissible requests. they should focus on substance, but let me emphasise that this is more than just a legal or practical discussion. it is an extremely political one as well. as you know, the current budget does not allow an increase in the human resources of the commission. any reallocation of resources would only be possible from other parts of the commission, and any new tasks would require internal redeployments and the identification of negative priorities elsewhere. we have to take this constraint into account and avoid that this review has a negative impact on other tasks of the commission that are crucial to deliver on our joint priorities, and here i’m thinking of both policy-related tasks and horizontal tasks such as implementation and enforcement. this is not an easy file, but it is a very important one, and we have a shared responsibility to deliver. i am looking forward to continuing our joint work on this file so that together we can bring the revision of the aarhus regulation to a successful and timely conclusion. the commission stands ready to assist the co-legislators in this process. thank you very much for your attention.
the rapporteur of the committee on legal affairs. mr president, allow me to continue with my predecessors, whose words i agree with. first of all, i would like to say that there is a real need to harmonize the form of the regulation and the international treaties themselves. in short, there are certain gaps between what the aarhus convention says and what our regulation says as a source of european law, which applies this international treaty to european law. and that was the main philosophy of my deliberations as the rapporteur of the juri committee. i understand that environmental protection and, say, public access to the environment and the ability of the non-profit sector to intervene in administrative and other processes is a sensitive political issue. that's obvious, that's how i see it, and there may be different political views on how broad the public's rights should be in this area. but i, as rapporteur, have really focused on the legal side of the matter, that is to say the compatibility of the international treaty and the compatibility of the international treaty with our european regulation. and i am glad that this procedure, which was carried over after long debates in the juri committee, is in fact part of the juri committee's resolution. i am also pleased with the report voted by the envi committee, where the approach is similar, and as i have seen the work of both committees, i am convinced that a compromise can be found on the final text. i agree with most of the material and consider it to be of high quality.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. the european parliament's environment committee has just adopted the new climate protection law. the rapporteur is the next speaker, jytte guteland. we've decided that by 2030, we're going to cut 55 percent of co2 and other greenhouse gases -- and that's by 2030, which is nine years from now, more than we've done in the last 30 years. we need to invest in high-speed trains, for example, to reduce air traffic and reduce the need for air traffic. we need investment in wind farms and many other things. the commissioner said this last week in committee. there are sometimes conflicts of interest. not everything that sounds green at first glance can be implemented at the same time. if the planning for these projects, which we need to do to reduce co2 and other greenhouse gases, takes too long, then we will not reach the targets. the epp was therefore sceptical about some of the demands made by other groups. too many lawsuits are delaying necessary climate protection projects. but christian doleschal has drawn up a good compromise, and i listened very carefully to what the commissioner said, because it will not be the last compromise. we must also work out a compromise with the commission and the council at the end of the day. the epp therefore supports the text without amendments. i thank all those who have helped, especially the rapporteur, mr christian doleschal. he has really done a masterpiece here as a new member of parliament. thank you very much. i hope for a large majority in the vote tomorrow.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. commissioner, what is it? it is indeed time that we, 20 years after we joined the aarhus convention, finally took steps to live up to our commitments. not only large companies, but also citizens, ngos and indigenous peoples must be given the opportunity to make their voices heard on environmental and climate issues in the eu. one example is the legal process that sami youth in sweden and families around europe have brought because they were directly affected by climate change. heatwaves, droughts or forest fires hit them directly, and they challenged previous eu climate targets. however, the eu court of justice did not hear the case. this proposal also provides individuals directly affected by environmental problems with access to justice and the opportunity to put pressure on the eu institutions. it's time to go. it is problematic that the eu today does not live up to its international conventions. given that the whole eu project is based on the principles of the rule of law, it is even worse that the fundamental right to a judicial remedy is not guaranteed. with the eu now world leader in environmental and climate issues, not least in terms of the green deal and the eu climate law, we must show that we ourselves live up to the international convention concerning the right to review environmental legislation. as the eu is now reshaping its environmental and climate policy in a more ambitious direction, the institutions must also follow suit. we cannot then continue to do what is directly contrary to our environmental laws, whether in the environmental field or not. it's time we showed once and for all that we live as we teach.

madam president, fiscalis is a useful cooperation in the field of taxation, since it helps to detect tax fraud while respecting the tax sovereignty of the member states. we know from the data that some member states have more problems with tax fraud than others. for example, the differences in the relative vat gap vary between the best and worst performing countries by almost 50 times. fiscalis should therefore not be limited to the modernisation of tax administration and the detection of fraud. the programme also has the potential to be a tool for the exchange of best practices between member states. technological modernisation of the tax administration is only one side of the coin. technology is a tool, but the key to success is using it as efficiently as possible.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. international trade in its current form also contributes significantly to the loss of biodiversity. this fact is confirmed by numerous studies. i therefore welcome the commission's recognition of this in its very ambitious biodiversity strategy. but what is sorely lacking, particularly in international trade, is that this insight is followed by concrete and effective action. it is not enough to aim at cautious adjustments to free trade agreements. the aim must be to change the international trading system in a way that is consistent with the interests of biodiversity. this means, for example, that trade in products that are proven to be harmful to biodiversity must be stopped. this also means that developing countries in particular need to be supported in any necessary restructuring of their agricultural, forestry or fisheries sectors. the recognition of the importance of biodiversity conservation must be followed by consistent action. this parliament has the opportunity tomorrow to take a first step by adopting the biodiversity report, but without diluting it.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. the fact is, we're using more and more land. it is also a fact that our land use needs to be better aligned with environmental and species protection. but to do that we must not treat farmers and forestry workers as a problem but as partners. central to this is a clear commitment to ownership. this must be the starting point of the european biodiversity strategy. the report of the environment committee unfortunately sounds more like we were trying to turn nature into a natural history museum. this has little to do with reality. first, management and biodiversity are not mutually exclusive - on the contrary. the traditional pond management model shows that both can be very beneficial when mutually agreed. secondly, land use is not only the basis of life in rural areas, but also makes an important contribution to climate protection through the production of food, materials and energy. the strategy should clearly recognise this.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. biodiversity is the foundation of our life. everything that lives on this planet is ultimately a result of biodiversity and is a result of nature's ability to adapt and change. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. but agriculture - we should never forget this - is also a source of biodiversity; the common agricultural policy is also about supporting agriculture, which is also the source of biodiversity. what i find very surprising is that the commission's communication on biodiversity does not mention the problem of large predators. i think that people simply do not want to talk about this subject, or to keep it quiet, or worse, to talk about it in a nice way, but that is not the way to do justice to it. because there's not just wolves and bears. there is also the right of farmers and the right of the animals they keep.

in writing. although we have managed to positively adjust several conditions of this step in the negotiation process between parliament and the governments of the member states in favour of our citizens, i myself cannot fully support the introduction of covid certificates for specific persistent reasons. while we claim that the alternative passes for free movement in the eu are vaccination and a certificate of cure or valid negative pcr or antigen tests, we have not guaranteed that their conditions are clear, the same and equivalent everywhere. while vaccination is free, testing does not have to be free, and we have no agreed upon uniform timelines for its validity. such facts not only reduce the added value of covid certificates, but also do not completely exclude the creation of at least partial economic or other discrimination against those who are not yet vaccinated or cannot, or are afraid to be vaccinated. to avoid disappointing the high expectations of the people, which are caused by politicians, the governments of all member states, including slovakia, must now quickly manage the process of putting these certificates into practice. on the contrary, they cannot afford to juggle the introduction of any additional border restrictions unless an urgent epidemiological situation requires it, the outbreaks of which they must inform in good time.

mr. chairman, it is natural that in public life we face a higher level of criticism, pressure, negative reactions. we encounter these phenomena regularly here in the european parliament and take them as a natural part of our work. we need, and i welcome, a critical view of our work. but death threats and threats to relatives, sexist innuendo, or the sending of degrading images must never be accepted, condoned, or tolerated in our work. however, such practices are all too common and familiar to many female colleagues in politics, active journalists, television reporters and public speakers. many of them choose to leave politics or withdraw from public life because of bullying. and that's just because they're women. there are at least two ways to improve the space for women in politics. today, we are working in several committees of this parliament on legislative changes to heal our digital environment so that what is not allowed offline is not allowed online. many people mistakenly think that the internet is a free-for-all. we have to change that view. the second is to speak out about the issues that women face in public life. to speak up and to take action when we see behavior that is unacceptable. and it's important that we men speak up. how women feel online, ladies and gentlemen, is something that affects all of us.
madam president, in the eu we keep 300 million livestock in cages every year. three hundred million creatures without the ability to behave as they are born to. they're doomed to a life of stress with no escape. antimicrobial resistance and zoonotic diseases are associated with this breeding. in the eu we have the highest standards of animal welfare, but they are still not sufficient. we consider it a good standard to increase the size of the cage or transport. extensive scientific research demonstrates that cage-keeping is contrary to the welfare of farmed animals and that none of the cage systems that have been designed so far provide space to meet the ethological needs of farmed animals. the transition to cage-free farming is a fundamental change in animal production. with the right policy set-up and support for our farmers, it is sustainable and creates full-time jobs in the region. there are alternative systems. they're economically successful. cell-free, organic and sustainable agriculture is a system that we must promote as a matter of priority for the sake of animal health, human health and complementarity with the planet. this is what eu citizens want, what scientists say, what good practice shows. if science is the basis for policy decisions, the european union has a chance to be an inspiration for the whole world in the field of livestock farming and welfare, just as it is in the field of climate and biodiversity.
author. – mr president, let me start with a story. kate is an eight—year—old child with middle hard autistic spectrum disorder. kate’s mother is a hairdresser from a very small slovak village, and she is a single mother. when kate was two and a half, she was diagnosed with autism by a paediatrician. ‘so what do i do now?’ her mother asked. ‘so now you have to find a therapist’ the doctor said. ‘where?’ – ‘well, that i cannot tell you. there are none available in our district’. as kate’s mother was looking for help among doctors, one of them advised her ‘you know, in this country it is better to place autistic children in an orphanage’. the closest therapist was 90 kilometres away from kate. there was no pre—school facility in their district that would accept autistic kate. a retired neighbour took care of kate while her mother was working, so the family was very low on money. the income hardly covered the rent and the babysitting. when the therapist finally called after a year on a waiting list, anna had to refuse the therapies because she did not have money for that. so now kate is in a special school. her mother has to drive her 40 kilometres every single day, and kate does not thrive. she actually struggles. she struggles at school and she struggles at home and she will struggle for the rest of her life. this is the sad result of unavailable therapies and early childhood intervention services. sadly, this is a very common picture of the situation of a family with autistic children in many member states. autism is a very complex, lifelong disability. each affected person experiences it differently. an estimated five million people in europe are on the autism spectrum, facing a high level of discrimination in their daily lives. people on the autism spectrum, even those considered to be high—functioning, have poor employment outcomes. it is unacceptable that the estimated employment rates for people with autism in europe is less than 10%, far below the employment rate of persons with disabilities. they are highly exposed to the risk of poverty and social exclusion. so we need to act now. we need to ensure that people on the autism spectrum have quality and inclusive employment with equal pay as for persons without disabilities. it is their right. therefore, i ask the commission what it intends to do to improve the prospects for personal development and the employment outcomes of autistic people, specifically with regard to protection and the legal frameworks covering social security, working conditions, minimum wages and non—discrimination in institutions. what concrete measures does the commission propose in order to take into account the quality of life, diversity of the situation and the needs of persons on the autism spectrum and the effects of the pandemic and of the intersection or discrimination they face in their daily lives? reasonable accommodation is key both in education and training as well as in employment. we need modified recruitment procedures. we need autism awareness training for employers and co—workers. this is absolutely essential. however, the lack of disaggregated data is a barrier to developing and evaluating effective policies and improving their situation. until 2015, no data on autism was collected at eu level. eurostat has still no data on autism. how concretely will the commission improve and ensure the collection and monitoring of quality disaggregated data of the employment situation of autistic people, including those with co—occurring conditions, other disabilities and in institutions and sheltered settings ? all persons on the autism spectrum have equal rights in all fields of life, and they are entitled to rights to dignity, equal treatment, independent living, autonomy and full participation in our societies, respecting and valuing their input to the social and economic progress in the eu. however, regrettably, there are still many obstacles to overcome in order to make this a reality. it is absolutely high time to act now.

on behalf of the left group. mr president, since the 1970s the european union has been setting environmental policy guidelines through environmental action programmes. these programmes have integrated positive considerations into key areas of environmental policy, such as biodiversity, climate or soils, but also trade policy or access to justice, among others. the social aspect must also be considered. all these issues require concrete measures and the parliament's position, which we are debating today, contributes to this. the positive elements which may result from this are in conflict with the neoliberal and mercantilist policies of the european union, which this proposal does not reject. this is exemplified by the perverse and ineffective market approaches that enshrine the right to pollute, such as emissions trading. many other examples could be recalled. a serious approach to environmental problems requires a society that is oriented towards the satisfaction of human needs rather than profit.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. we must act decisively and wisely to protect our environment. i would like to thank all those who have contributed to this report, and especially mrs agnès evren, who has been a member of the epp. the first priority of the new environment action programme is rightly the objective of achieving climate neutrality in the european union. we are the first generation to suffer the consequences of climate change. we have to look not only at our forests, but also at the spread of deserts in many parts of the world and many other things. we are the last generation that can stop climate change in a sustainable way, because there are dangerous points, tipping points in the climate system. and if we don't do that now, then our children and grandchildren are not likely to be able to control climate change. that is why it is good that the commission will be making its proposal for fit for 55 next week. and i appeal on behalf of the epp that we rely above all on the market economy, on incentives, on social equality. we want the emissions trading system to be at the heart of european climate policy. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. and he has no way of balancing the burdens at european level. that is why i think that incentives, a market economy, individual creativity, that is the way to go, and not too many regulations from brussels.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the eu as a region is a world leader in policies to protect our nature and our environment. it is an important tradition that since the 1970s we have had environmental action programmes as guiding principles for environmental policy. for the eighth time, we are now going to adopt such a programme and it should guide us properly. it should set out visions and ensure that all parties take their responsibilities. we must now accelerate through the green given. we also need to meet the un's sustainable development goals. i am therefore pleased that the european parliament is now taking a progressive role in the final negotiations with the member states. today we take a stand on new indicators of well-being beyond gdp. today, we are taking the fight for an end date for the use of taxpayer funds for fossil subsidies as early as 2025. today we take a big step towards the just transition to the sustainable welfare economy.
mr president, dear colleagues, and europe: this is the first time i speak to you in this parliament. and i will address what concerns me the most: i will address climate change. with 73% of fossil energy in europe’s energy system, this environmental action programme that we have in front of us today gives us a unique chance to end the fossil subsidies and end a fossil era. and i have a clear message to give to you today, and let me be very clear: (the speaker remained silent for 30 seconds.) no more words can save us now – we need climate action.
mr president, the ambitious green deal needs an ambitious environmental action programme, because indeed we have a problem. if you look at it from economic perspective, can you imagine running the european monetary union on a 300% deficit? that’s what we’re doing with the planet. and i think this is where we really need to act on a number of fields. i want to point out one in particular, and that is soils. and i would like to also emphasise that even here in the environmental action programme, in a legislative text, the parliament calls for a binding legal framework for soils because it’s one of the elements that is missing, and i think this is something which we need for biodiversity, the climate, hazardous chemicals, for supporting our farmers. to be able to deliver on the action programme we also need sufficient resources of the commission. and that’s something that is lacking, not only at the commission, but also at the agencies. so we have the solid science and we have the capacity to not only make the rules, but to deliver and monitor them. so i hope that, not like today, tabling amendments for more resources, next year we will see a sufficiently resourced commission to deliver on this action programme.
mr president, one of the main lessons of this pandemic is that we need to invest in research and innovation and to increase our strategic resilience. in the light of the pandemic i welcome the ‘era vs corona’ action plan that calls for a deepening of cooperation, data sharing and coordination of research and innovation and funding against the coronavirus between the commission and the member states. progress made since 2000 in era’s ambition to create a genuine single market for research is visible. but we should have got much further, and there are still large disparities between member states. recent developments with the virus mutations clearly proved european money should be invested in long-term solutions such as effective and innovatory diagnostics, treatments and medicines. we have to support wide clinical trials for management of coronavirus patients, including those suffering from long covid and post-covid symptoms that occur in more than one third of cases. we need to show our citizens that their health is our priority.
for the ecr group. madam president, it is the case that the report on the review of the macroeconomic legislative framework by the econ committee, a report which is largely concerned with the reform of fiscal rules in the european union, mentions the word 'consolidation' only once, and only in the following sentence: the european parliament calls for the prevention of the premature consolidation of public finances. end of quote. it's as if we really believe that rising public debt is a permanent source of wealth growth, and as if we stopped saying what we used to say before this crisis, that in good times, we should reduce debt. i agree that consolidation should be timed for a period of good times after the pandemic, i.e. when stable economic growth is restored. but the preparation of consolidation plans in the member states should start today, so that we are ready to start consolidation as soon as the general escape clause is deactivated. governments should identify concrete consolidation measures today that will increase the sustainability of public finances in the long term. the basis for such a list should be the identification of inefficient expenditure, otherwise the only lesson to be learned from this crisis will be that higher public debt today means higher taxes tomorrow. as the econ report does not call on the member states to prepare for consolidation, i cannot support it.
madam president, i say to the house and the commissioner: the post-pandemic re-opening of the economies is followed by increased demand for raw materials. current market conditions include huge supply chain disruption and sudden price increase. and so sectorial stakeholders call upon the member states and, especially, the commission to proceed with the appropriate action. some of them, i would say very important, have been already addressed and adopted. however, challenges of the sector cannot be a reason not to reach our energy and climate targets. remember that the construction sector accounts for one third of eu waste. we must accelerate the transition of the sector towards a greener, sustainable model, as greater material efficiency could save up to 80% of the emissions produced. we need to reduce our strategic dependence. here, the launch of the alliances for batteries and hydrogen are good examples to be followed. we should not forget also about the 18 million jobs in the eu that are linked to the construction sector, and to minimise negative side effects of the transition by creating other sustainable jobs and reskilling for those who will be affected.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, first of all, i didn’t plan to speak in plenary this week, but i had to replace a colleague of mine, to whom i wish a full and fast recovery from his injuries. but now to the topic. commissioner, without a doubt, the commission has been investing significantly in very promising, innovative research projects. they have shown the possibilities of advanced non-animal models. at the same time, however, the investments in these innovative methods are still dwarfed in comparison to the current investments in animal studies. in 2017, over 23 million animals were impacted by science. the majority of them were bred and killed without actually being used in the experiments. it all happens despite specific provisions in reach to promote alternative methods and to only use animals as a last resort. today, the eu has major challenges to face: infectious diseases, the fight against cancer and ensuring a clean and safe environment. these challenges are complex and the eu is addressing them on many fronts, one of them research and testing. the rapid emergence of advanced non—animal models offer immense opportunities to replace animals and improve research. the joint research centre has listed many of these methods for several disease areas, but they have not yet been followed by concrete measures. what we are asking from the commission is to do more of what it already does, but in a coordinated manner and with concrete goals that can replace animals in specific scientific areas. targeted funding, education and broad collaborations are key to making innovative advanced models and technologies the new normal. the commission should work towards inter—agency alignment by setting up a dialogue on the regulatory use of non—animal models in anticipation of the ‘one substance, one assessment’ approach, and to ensure a proper share of the agency’s budget is dedicated to these models.
author. – mr president, thank you very much for giving me the floor. dear colleagues, we all know that the covid-19 pandemic has severely affected the eu aviation sector, with the loss of more than two thirds of traffic and many many jobs, as carriers seek to maintain viability and reduce the costs. despite the state aid schemes, most of the airlines are still struggling to return to pre-pandemic activity levels. despite some improvements regarding the travel restrictions and the vaccination campaign, it is feared that the difficulties that the airline sectors are facing could lead to more job cuts and the further downgrading of terms and conditions. therefore, it is our duty to ensure fair competition. it is our duty to ensure that companies receiving public financial support fully respect workers’ rights and labour laws. against this background, the committee on employment and social affairs has the following questions to the commission. taking into account the evaluation of regulation (ec) no 1008/2008 how will the commission ensure effective enforcement of applicable eu legislation? what legal measures are being considered to ensure a level playing field and legal certainty for air crews, including the requirement to know which labour law is applicable to air crews? second, when will the commission put forward a revision of regulation (ec) no 1008/2008 in order to ensure decent working and employment conditions in the aviation sector? third, when will the commission publish the findings of the ad hoc group of member states’ experts on aviation and labour law? how will it remedy the lack of reliable data on the number of aircrew jobs, outsourced jobs and agency staff?
mr president, i would like to thank you. my colleagues! the ipcc report is a reminder that we have waited far too long and that we still have sectors where emissions are increasing. it's a reminder of what we should have done 20 or 30 years ago. as legislators, we must have courage. we have to do whatever it takes to stay below 1.5 degrees of warming. i've said it before, and i'll say it again: the eu emissions trading system is europe's most important tool for reducing emissions. it's an effective marketing tool to get us all the way. as the negotiator in charge of renew europe, i promise to do everything i can to ensure that we have the most effective tool possible. but i know there are people in this room who say they want the best for the climate, but at the same time they say they want to delay the timetable, who at the same time say it's going to be too expensive, who at the same time say it's going to be too risky, and who at the same time say that emissions reductions should be done somewhere else. fellas, we must be brave and do what is necessary!
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the latest ipcc report from last august makes it very clear that to stop global warming we must stop emitting carbon urgently. we are at a point of no return, there is a margin but it is a concrete and unambiguous margin. the 30 years we have given ourselves until 2050 cannot be a test, the challenge is huge because it concerns everything: the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the information provided by the member states. we have a first deadline, 2030, when governments and the union at the helm must specify what they will do: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we welcome the adoption of the fit for 55 but we are aware that the changes to be promoted are radical and that a total and just transformation is needed. that is why we will fight to ensure that the changes we have to make do not harm the most vulnerable citizens and regions. we shall be following closely the establishment of the social fund. moreover, the resources we will get from the implementation of the cbam should be devoted to these most fragile sectors of the population.
mr president, mr vice-president, one of the key elements of the 'fit for 55' package is undoubtedly the carbon adjustment mechanism at our borders. this centrepiece of our climate policy has a very noble purpose, which i obviously support: exporting our climate ambitions while maintaining the competitiveness of our industries. this would prevent their relocation. but at this stage, mr vice-president, i must make it clear that i have serious reservations about whether the draft of the cbam, as presented on 14 july, can actually meet these objectives. with the planned end of free allowances, which is obviously inevitable, it will be our companies that will lose competitiveness on third-party markets. it will be our domestic companies that will bear the additional administrative burden of the cbam, and it will be our manufacturing companies that will have to pay the co2 price of all those who produce with a lower or non-existent carbon price. it is inconceivable and unacceptable to me that our companies now have to pay for the lack of ambition of others. would it not be more logical, mr vice-president, that the polluter himself should pay?
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the fit for 55 package is the key instrument for achieving the new co2 reduction target by 2030. parliament had called for a 60% reduction, but we are ready to roll up our sleeves to help meet the main challenge of our century, which is to accompany the recovery towards a new model of sustainable development with zero emissions. but it must be clear that this change must be accompanied by a just transition that leaves no one behind. you can never have environmental sustainability without social sustainability. it is clear that the green transition cannot be for those who can afford it and the costs cannot fall on the economically most vulnerable. that is why we think that great attention should be paid to the social impact and costs of extending the ets system to public transport and housing and why we believe that the new social climate fund is going in the right direction, accompanying the transition with equity so that it is for everyone. the work on the package that we are facing in the coming months will certainly be demanding, but this cannot be an alibi for us to stand by and leave things as they are. the latest ipcc data has shown once again that a clear and rapid response is essential to avoid a transition from an emergency... i think it is extremely important that we base our political decisions on scientific facts. i therefore think it is very good that there is an international working group of climate scientists advising us. clearly, climate change is man-made and poses a major threat to humans, animals and the environment. the last report states that the greenhouse gas methane dissolves in the atmosphere after 12 years. this is unlike co2. with a constant emission of methane, the atmospheric layer of methane does not thicken or thin. the report of the intergovernmental panel on climate change (ipcc) also states that the impact of constant methane emissions on climate change is overestimated by three to four times. my question to the commission is this: what impact does this have on climate legislation for agriculture, for example? should not agriculture be seen as part of the solution rather than the problem?

mr president, fit for 55 is a much needed package. the current status quo is not enough for europe to reach climate neutrality by 2050. if we want to achieve what we pledged, we need to go further. with this proposal the eu addresses climate change in several sectors. previous interventions focused mainly on energy production and industry. this is a step in the right direction. on the other hand, there is room for improvement. we need a better connection between the climate agenda and research and development. fit for 55 is comprehensive when it comes to regulation, standards, taxation and carbon pricing, but it falls short when it comes to the development of clean, affordable technologies. horizon europe partnerships are just an example of an initiative that could help us to develop the technological resources that we need to achieve our goals. we want a package linked to the real world, one that ensures a fair distribution of the burden we will put on different sectors of society, paying special attention to our citizens, counting on existing and emerging technologies, and contributing to the creation of wealth and jobs.

mr president, i would like to thank you. a few days ago, i was talking to a man in the shipping business. he was supposed to buy new ships. but he was in doubt: what technology should he bet on? can he count on there being enough green fuel? when will he have to pay for the pollution of the ships, and how much will it really cost? he was insecure, and i can understand that. a lot of european companies are facing the same kind of thinking right now. there are many unknowns in climate policy, as it is now. we'll change that when we succeed in landing an ambitious climate package. i know we can, but i am sincerely afraid that political apathy and all sorts of national considerations will drag things out. but time, it's not a luxury we have. if we do not act quickly, we risk slowing down the transformation of those companies that are ready. we risk missing out on the green investments we could have made, and worst of all, we risk not addressing the climate challenge in time. so let's get to it!

in writing. the package of proposals, known as fit for 55, is a good kick-off from the european commission to the very difficult negotiations that now await us in the european parliament and among member states to agree effective measures to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and achieve our climate goals. some of the proposed measures, while very ambitious, will encounter strong resistance in several member states, as well as here in the european parliament. in particular, the proposed extension of emission allowance trading to buildings and road transport. many question whether it is a suitable tool to help reduce greenhouse gas emissions, and whether its consequences will not cause even worse social unrest than the recent yellow vest movement in france. without well-thought-out and timely accompanying measures, such measures will have a negative impact on the energy bills paid by citizens as well as on the fuel prices of their cars. in devastating ways, it can especially affect those from the most vulnerable social strata. i am afraid that the climate social fund and especially its budget in the form proposed will not be sufficient on its own to offset these social consequences. let us not forget that we are already facing a significant increase in energy prices, albeit for other reasons.

mr president, the ipa iii is a significant financial instrument designed for future eu member states. therefore, it deserves full political attention across europe. congratulations to ms zovko and mr picula for negotiating an agreement which improves governance and increases scrutiny by this house, including through establishing a high—level geopolitical dialogue. ipa iii is much more than a financial transfer. it is a political instrument which binds our partners in the region to put fundamentals first, and to work diligently on rule of law reforms and sound and sustainable public investment. money alone, however, will not guarantee that things move forward in the western balkans. ipa iii must go hand in hand with europe’s political ambition, strategic commitment and geopolitical shrewdness when dealing with countries to which we clearly offered a european perspective as long as two decades ago. it is therefore high time we all – and member states especially – do much more for a successful european union enlargement.
mr. chairman, every crisis is an opportunity, but only if we have the courage to make the big move. our recovery plan commits member states to invest in digital infrastructure and sustainability. we must seize this opportunity to develop new economic models that will create new and more sustainable jobs. however, we must not forget the social dimension of the recovery, as in most of the european union prices are rising and wages are staying the same. let's not forget that some fat people took advantage of this crisis and profited from it. the big multinational companies, known for violating the rights of their workers and our privacy rights, have made a lot of money. yet, they still skillfully evade paying taxes. it is unacceptable that digital giants pay a tax rate many times lower than, for example, small and medium-sized enterprises. we need to ensure a global response to destroy tax havens. we cannot afford to let the big guys miss out on over 825 billion euros a year, but that's why our citizens are getting regular warnings. it is not the citizens who have to pay for the consequences of the crisis, and in conclusion: this crisis has shown that there is no turning back. the european union must have a permanent financial mechanism for crises. there is a need to revise european rules to reduce the disparities between member states, between rich and poor.
mr president, i want to say that you are indeed right that the eu has weathered the past year better than many had expected. we caught up in terms of vaccination rates with leading countries around the world and our economic recovery is underway. of course, we still have a way to go to defeat covid both in europe and abroad, but the eu has indeed proved many of its doubters wrong. looking ahead, though, what myself and many of my colleagues are especially concerned about is the continuing democratic backsliding in several member states. president von der leyen spoke about various ambitions that the eu should pursue, from healthcare to military capacity, from climate change to social policy. of course, i support all these ambitions, but the success of any of these future projects relies on a shared set of principles and values. none of these programmes that the president outlined will be successful and work well in the long run if the european union turns into a disjointed club of democracies and other regime types. the trust we need, the mutual trust we need, to sustain these initiatives and the shared sense of purpose to drive them will evaporate if this is not the case. what we’re seeing today – the suppression of critical voices, state-sponsored hate campaigns, attacks on independent institutions – all of this suggests that some members of our community are increasingly drifting away. the eu must recognise that the orchestrators of these illiberal developments are first and foremost government elites, while citizen support for the eu remains very high in both hungary and poland. this is why the european commission needs to take action as we have called for. it needs to put up a fight for its values, because if we don’t, we will never have the political strength and the cohesion to move forward with these ambitious projects. therefore, president von der leyen, rule of law and democracy is not just another agenda among many; it’s not just another box to tick; it’s not just another paragraph in a speech; it’s a precondition for anything else we want to do together.
on behalf of the ecr group. – madam president, i fully support the exchange of tax information and i am against tax evasion. however, we are to some extent again in a situation where the report turned into a political fight over the questions of tax harmonisation in the european union. i believe national tax sovereignty should be maintained. tax harmonisation in the union might result in the limitation of tax competition. i believe healthy tax competition has benefits compared to full tax harmonisation, as it enables member states to compare and apply best practices of the other member states. cuts on inefficient spending can allow governments to buy more for the same money or to use the savings to reduce the tax burden to attract international investment and stimulate economic growth and innovation. we should not limit this option yet.
on behalf of the id group. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the israeli pegasus phone-spying software was supposed to be used to fight crime, but states have used it to spy on journalists, business leaders and even more than 600 politicians, including perhaps french president emmanuel macron. in the face of this scandal, several national courts decided to open judicial investigations. i hope, without being overly optimistic, that they will be able to identify and punish those responsible. the right to privacy, freedom of the press and respect for the sovereignty of certain states, such as france, are essential principles that have been seriously violated. beyond these investigations, however, we need to take steps to restrict the sale of this type of spyware and to strengthen our cybersecurity to try and prevent such a scandal from happening again. pending such international regulation, i suggest that the european union strengthen its regulation of 5 may 2009 on the control of exports of goods with dual civil and military use and recommend that the states concerned accede to the 1996 wassenaar arrangement on the same subject. the union has already announced a new cybersecurity strategy. i shall judge it by its results and not by its intentions: the union has sinned too often in its naiveté.
on behalf of the left group. the commission is not prepared to accept this. pegasus, the winged horse of greek mythology, turns out to be a trojan horse with evil intentions. in short, such software must be withdrawn from circulation. and since the nsa scandal, at the latest, we all know what espionage can do. and the private spy and hacking service provider nso group, which sells this software to dozens of surveillance-hungry countries and doesn't even think about stopping, needs to be shown the red card. these services are illegal. and that they're still taking taxpayer money to use to unfetteredly monitor unwanted female journalists, human rights activists, lawyers, that's really the epitome of perversion. the commission and the council are not the only ones to have been concerned about this, but i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has been involved in the preparation of the draft directive on the protection of personal data. ladies and gentlemen of the council and commission, you should ensure that no member state uses such software. ladies and gentlemen, we're not done with this.
madam president, the pegasus scandal is deeply troubling. it calls for european action on several levels. first, we must focus our joint efforts much more on safe communication. we must adapt to the new technological realities and ensure that journalists, politicians and activists across the european union have reliable means to a secure exchange of information. democratic politics and free and independent media cannot flourish when both interpersonal and professional relations get raked at a massive scale with the help of malicious activity. let’s remember: if journalists cannot rely on secure communication, their lives can be in danger. in slovakia, we learned the hardest way when ján kuciak was murdered. second, we must ask the european commission to follow up thoroughly, and investigate on both the potential and/or the alleged abuse of digital surveillance technology by certain member states. it is unacceptable that modern technologies are misused within europe with the aim of undermining our freedom and democracy. it is good that, after our discussion on the pegasus scandal in inge committee, we are addressing this issue in the plenary today. however, subsequent european action on this matter is going to be crucial. dear colleagues, if we allow dictatorships, such as china, to become permanent technological forerunners, european democracy will be at dire risk. there is still time to join forces to prevent this bleak picture of our own future.
member of the commission. madam president, mr minister, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you again for this debate and to return to some points. first, i have heard a number of questions, including the recall, sometimes, of written questions already asked in recent weeks or months. i will of course urge the services and my colleagues to provide answers to the questions that are asked when we have the information. on the substance, the pegasus case has once again demonstrated - and many have pointed out - the urgency of the rapid adoption of the eprivacy regulation, which will strengthen the confidentiality of communications, as well as the need - as i mentioned earlier - for the commission's recommendation to protect journalists. and we will have to check the implementation of these recommendations by the member states. i believe that this will be an important element in the measures we will take gradually to strengthen this protection, particularly that of journalists. but they were not the only ones in this kind of situation. let me also underline that we do have eu rules in place for criminal liability. should software, such as pegasus, be used inappropriately, eu law requires member states to criminalise the use of it under their domestic legal framework. the directive on attacks against information systems provides definitions of criminal offences and sanctions for a number of offences, including illegal access to information systems, illegal interference with systems and data, illegal interception and the illegal distribution of tools to commit such offences. this includes the misuse of the personal data of another person identity fraud. let me remind you that the enforcement of criminal law, or of data protection rules, is in the hands of national authorities. as i said during my introduction, the hungarian data protection authority has opened an investigation into the pegasus case. the commission will of course follow the procedure very closely, and you know that we are taking the initiative in different member states to ensure the independence of the authority in charge of data protection. and of course, we will also ask the hungarian authority to come forward with transparency about the results of the investigations. i have said what the normal timelines for that are, but again, we continue to monitor it. you know that, in all european regulations, there's a real disincentive to installing independent bodies in the member states to organise enforcement of those rules. and in conclusion, i would like to say that this survey is obviously particularly important in the specific case of the use of software. but again, it shows the need to move forward on a number of the work that the commission has proposed to you, and to the council, to strengthen the rules we have in this area and to ask the member states to implement national provisions which allow prosecutions to go much further, particularly - as i have mentioned - in criminal prosecutions which can sometimes be brought in cases like this. i thank you again for the debate we have had today, but we will continue to follow very closely all developments in the various aspects of this pegasus case in the coming weeks and months.
