in writing. there is no doubt that the pegasus spyware case is an international scandal that cannot remain without significant consequences. extensive investigative journalism and investigations in several countries since then have proved that spyware can be used by certain governmental or other organizations to spy on journalists, opposition politicians, or even government leaders. it is important to note that, according to the information we have at present, only the current hungarian government, led by viktor orbán, has this software among the member states of the european union. the fact is that fidesz is using all possible means to prevent the investigation of the use of the software in hungary, hinders the work of the special committee of the hungarian parliament, does not release data of public interest in the case to the public illegally. we know that the orbán government has allowed record numbers of surveillance in the past, but they are hiding all the information about it. the very fact that a european union country is involved in this serious international scandal must have its consequences. the orbán government's meddling in this matter is also a serious threat to the european community. that is why i am calling on the eu institutions to use all means possible to pressure the orbán government to stop using the pegasus software and to prove, with adequate guarantees, that it has not used the spyware to conduct unethical surveillance of journalists, politicians or even european partners. transparency is not only in the interest of the hungarian people, but also of the european community!
the next item is the debate on the council and commission statements on media freedom and further deterioration of the rule of law in poland (2021/2880(rsp)).
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, honourable members, commissioner, pluralism and media freedom are key for any free and democratic society. as citizens of the european union, we know well that they are not self-evident. they are rather the result of the historic struggle for fundamental rights and freedoms, namely for the freedom of thought and the freedom of expression. the two freedoms reinforce all other human rights and fundamental freedoms, allowing society to develop and progress. media freedom and pluralism is, and must remain, a central pillar of the european project based on the promotion and protection of the rule of law. as the presidency, we devote our special attention to equal respect for the fundamental values and principles of the eu, including media freedom. this is a joint responsibility of the institutions and the member states. together, we have to ensure that independent, free and diverse media thrive and that citizens have access to reliable information. this is the basis of any functioning democracy: to allow for the exchange of diverse and even conflicting opinions to contribute to constructive, rational and open democratic debates. the covid-19 pandemic has exacerbated numerous pre-existing challenges for media and journalists, such as the sharp decline in revenues and the various transformations brought by the digital environment. yet a free, independent and pluralistic media system has never been as important as now to ensure that our citizens can make informed decisions, relying on objective and diverse sources of information. the slovenian presidency believes that the freedom, plurality and professionalism of the media and the fight against fake news deserve special attention. in this context, the council is committed to working on strengthening media freedom and pluralism and is looking forward to the presentation by the commission of the different initiatives set out in the european democracy action plan. media pluralism and freedom constitute one of the four pillars of the rule-of-law report prepared by the commission. the 2021 report highlights some important progress in this area, such as the strengthening of the independence of media regulators, including in the context of the implementation of the audiovisual media services directive, but also underlines areas that deserve further improvement, such as the protection of journalists and the transparency of the media ownership. the issue related to media freedom as a constituent part of the rule of law are regularly addressed in the council. the presidency remains committed to addressing these issues in the context of the council’s annual rule-of-law dialogue, initiated by the german presidency in 2019. on 19 october at the general affairs council, we will have a horizontal discussion on rule-of-law matters on the basis of the 2021 rule-of-law report, which will be followed up by the country-specific discussions with ministers of european affairs on 23 november concerning five member states, namely croatia, cyprus, italy, latvia and lithuania. in addition, the european commission continues to closely monitor the recent rule-of-law developments in member states. as the presidency, we always urge for the principle that equal criteria apply to everyone. this is vital to maintain citizens’ trust in public institutions and a prerequisite for mutual trust among the member states.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, thank you for having put on the agenda this point on the situation of media freedom and rule of law in poland. i am pleased to update you on the recent developments since the july plenary, where you discussed the rule-of-law situation in poland last time. i was myself in poland two weeks ago on the occasion of the commemoration of the events of august 1980 and i reminded the polish people and the polish authorities that we need a strong poland at the core of the eu, shaping it on the basis of our common values and mutual trust. allow me to start now this introduction with media-related aspects. when media freedom and pluralism are under threat, democracy itself is under threat. the 2021 rule-of-law report found several aspects of concern regarding media freedom and pluralism, be it as regards the media market, the role of national authorities, as well as the environment for journalists’ work and their safety. we fear that the so-called strategy of re-polonisation of the media goes against our values – media freedom and pluralism – and also against our internal market. this is why we covered the new draft media law, also known as lex tvn, and the situation of polska press in our 2021 report, and i have heard many concerns from many sides on this draft law. i also note that the polish senate rejected the bill last week on thursday, 9 september. if this law is ultimately adopted, we would need to assess its compliance with eu law and in particular the right to freedom of establishment. more generally, when it comes to the media environment, there should be no political pressure, and journalists should have the right conditions to do their job. we all gain respect through our actions, accepting a diversity of opinions and allowing citizens to be duly informed. commissioner breton and i are working to design a european media freedom act that was mentioned by president von der leyen in her speech this morning. the aim is to protect the media as key actors in our democracies and stop those who threaten media freedom. this means having an eu law for the first time ever, with safeguards to promote the independence and pluralism of the media. honourable members, let me now refer to some other rule-of-law developments in poland concerning the judiciary. the latest developments in poland are particularly worrying. you will remember that in april this year, the commission referred poland to the court of justice regarding the new law on the judiciary of december 2019 and the continued activities of the disciplinary chamber of the polish supreme court. the commission considers that the law on the judiciary undermines the independence of polish judges and is incompatible with the primacy of eu law. besides referring the infringement case to the court of justice, the commission also asked the court to order interim measures. the objective was to prevent the aggravation of serious and irreparable harm to judicial independence in the eu legal order. on 14 july the court granted all the interim measures requested by the commission. furthermore, in the context of another infringement procedure, which the commission referred to the court of justice in october 2019, the court found on 15 july that the disciplinary regime for judges in poland is not compatible with eu law. among other things, the court of justice found that the disciplinary chamber of the polish supreme court does not provide all the guarantees of impartiality and independence and is not protected from the direct or indirect influence of the polish legislature and executive. as a follow-up, on 20 july commissioner reynders sent poland a letter asking for information as regards compliance with these rulings. we received the reply from poland on 16 august. unfortunately, the reply of poland was not satisfactory, and these two european court of justice rulings regarding the independence of polish judges have not been fully implemented in poland. the developments on the ground are particularly worrying. the disciplinary chamber continues to schedule and examine cases concerning judicial immunities and disciplinary liability of judges. also, there have been no measures to suspend the effects of the decisions already taken to lift the immunity of judges by the disciplinary chamber, as required by the interim order of the court of justice. furthermore, on 26 august the disciplinary officer appointed by the polish minister of justice informed about disciplinary investigations launched against a judge who applied the interim measures order of the court of justice. on 9 september the minister of justice decided to suspend the judge concerned for one month. given that the actions of the polish authorities continue to undermine the functioning of the polish justice system, the commission thus took two separate decisions last week to ensure the implementation of the decisions of the court of justice. first, the commission decided to request that the court of justice impose financial penalties on poland to ensure compliance with the court’s interim measures order of 14 july. the commission is asking the court to impose a daily penalty payment on poland for as long as the measures imposed by the court’s order are not fully implemented. second, the commission also decided to send a letter of formal notice to poland for not taking the necessary measures to comply fully with the judgment of the court of justice of 15 july, finding that polish law on the disciplinary regime against judges is not compatible with eu law. further concerns have emerged, in particular as regards the questioning of the primacy of eu law by the polish authorities. in that respect, on 14 july the polish constitutional tribunal issued a decision finding interim measures orders of the court of justice in the area of justice to be ultra vires. on 29 july the polish prosecutor general asked the polish constitutional tribunal to declare the unconstitutionality of article 6 of the european convention on human rights, which notably provides for the right to a tribunal established by the law insofar as it is applied to the constitutional tribunal. moreover, the constitutional tribunal is still called upon to rule in a case initiated by the polish prime minister, in which the principle of the primacy of eu law has been questioned. commissioner reynders also has addressed a letter to the polish authorities regarding this case and underlined the concerns that this motion raises in relation to the principle of sincere cooperation and the rule of law. as stated by president von der leyen in her speech this morning, i quote: ‘our values are guaranteed by our legal order and safeguarded by the judgments of the european court of justice. these judgments are binding. we make sure that they are respected, and we do so in every member state of our union.’ end of quote. honourable members, i can assure you that the commission will continue to act with determination to defend the need to have independent courts and effective judicial review everywhere in the eu. i thank you for your attention and look forward to your interventions.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, we should not have to have these debates. the checks and balances that are expected in every member state by every citizen should ensure that issues like media freedom and the rule of law, that are so fundamental to the legitimacy of our european project, are not subject to debate. member states are not all the same. we do not want them to be all the same. europe is about celebrating differences, but it can only work in harmony because we are bound by our common values system. a system that, if allowed to come under threat anywhere, puts us all at risk. and this is why it is correct to take action because this joint project of ours is only as strong as our defence of european values. and when polish citizens look to the european union, they expect the promise for europe to deliver for them, too. you cannot have a free and open democracy when state—controlled media freedom feeds streams of propaganda, when independent media is intimidated, when judges’ independence is threatened. and we in europe know how slippery the slope can be when loyalty to the party is placed above loyalty to the nation, above meritocracy, above truth. and so we must act and we must do so in a way that draws a difference between the polish authorities and the polish people. poland’s place is not on the sidelines of europe because the ideals that poland embodied when my generation was growing up inspired by lech wałęsa speaking of justice, law and equality, or st john paul forcing a change in the axes of global history, that is the poland that we know. that is the poland that we look to for inspiration. a european poland that understands that the fight for democracy and freedom did not end with the revolution and that the struggle is continuous and is constant. so my appeal is one: don’t move further away from these ideals that moved a continent only a generation ago, that changed the world. my appeal is: please come back.
i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals, but i would like to say that the commission has already taken a number of steps to ensure that the commission's proposals are implemented. polish once. poland again. the european parliament has once again voted in favour of a resolution tabled by no less than five parliamentary groups in this house, five pro-european groups identified with european values and which once again secure a majority for a resolution reiterating the european parliament's concern at the deterioration of the rule of law in poland. 'further deterioration' means a further turn of the screw in the continuing erosion of the rule of law, which is reflected in its latest episodes in this emergency resolution on freedom of expression: an initiative in the sejm, rejected by the polish senate and therefore timely for the ruling of this european parliament, which reiterates the importance of respecting information pluralism 'media pluralism' threatened again by a law that seeks to restrict that pluralism under the pretext of intervening in the national or foreign ownership of the media in poland. but i also reiterate my concern about judicial independence and the primacy of european law. an absolute majority, the law and justice majority, has launched a legislative strategy which has left no puppet in the polish constitutional building with its head on the wall as regards judicial independence: constitutional court, supreme court, judicial council and disciplinary chamber. but there have been initiatives before the european court of human rights and the court of justice which have declared the disciplinary chamber illegal under european law. and what do we see? that the minister of justice, who is also the prosecutor general, should ask the polish constitutional court to declare that the polish constitutional court is not a court within the meaning of article 6 of the european convention on human rights and therefore not within the meaning of article 47 of the european charter of fundamental rights. it is the last frontier, a flagrant disregard not only for the judgments of the court but also for the commitment to respect the law which the court of justice guarantees, in accordance with article 19 of the treaty on european union. it is therefore a qualitative step in the disregard for european law, which has been demonstrated time and again by the resolutions of the european parliament. but there is more: there is a call to the commission to activate article 6 of the rule of law conditionality, which is a regulation in force since 1 january of this year 2021, and which ensures that access to european funds and budgets must be linked to strict compliance with the rule of law. we welcome the fact that the commission has launched infringement proceedings. we welcome the commission's announcement that it is suspending access to react funds, which are cohesion funds, for municipalities that have infamously declared themselves 'lgbti-free zones', free of alleged lgbti ideology. but we also ask the commission to bear in mind the requirement to continue to apply - (the president withdrew the floor to the speaker)
on behalf of the renew group. – madam president, captured courts, attacks on independent media, hate against sexual minorities, public broadcasters blasting pro—government propaganda, violations of parliamentary procedures – this, unfortunately, is poland in the hands of the current government. by all standards, democracy and the rule of law in poland are in retreat, and actually have been for years. and the response of the european union so far has been mostly meagre: there have been a couple of court cases, but they fizzled out through the polish government’s persistent non—compliance, and this absolutely must change. obviously, there is a time for dialogue, but the time seems to be up, and access to eu funding must be conditional on compliance with european rules and european values. and even if that does not change the minds of the polish and hungarian governments, at least it will remind everybody else, including polish and hungarian citizens that the eu still stands for fundamental principles. the commission is therefore well within its rights to also request penalty payments for ongoing compliance and non—compliance with european court of justice (ecj) decisions. it’s a move that we’ve been – in this parliament – calling for for a long time. also, the government’s active campaign against independent media is equally deserving of eu attention. we support the call by vice—president jourová to introduce a media freedom act that would specifically protect this crucial sector from political interference. but in the meantime, all other legal steps must also be taken to prevent further centralisation of power in poland, including the suspension of eu funding because the eu cannot be sponsoring an assault on democracy and rule of law in its member state.

author. – mr president, dear colleagues, dear commissioner schmit, i don’t know where you get all the energy actually at this time, you know. i’m a big admirer. ladies and gentlemen, the economic crisis that was caused by the covid-19 pandemic has had a dramatic impact on the lives of all of us, especially on the vulnerable groups. it has led to deepening inequalities. it has led to increasing numbers of people that are at risk of poverty. it has shed a light on existing imbalances and accelerated dangerous social trends, especially on the vulnerable groups. this past year we have, fortunately, slowly started recovering from the detrimental social and economic effects, but joint efforts and strong political will are still needed to ensure a just and equitable recovery that will leave no one behind in the social europe that we all want to create and build. therefore, i ask the commission, how will you work together with the member states to address the social consequences of the pandemic, protect the rights and improve the situation of the most vulnerable groups? for the ecr group. mr president, i understand the concern that we would be handing over to algorithms the control of decisions that could seriously affect human lives. for example, because algorithms can make mistakes. but i do not agree that on the basis of such concerns alone we should ban the use of artificial intelligence altogether wherever it may have a legal impact on individuals, as this report proposes. we should have analytics that honestly compare the functionality and efficiency of human and algorithmic decision making, and make better decisions for that. in this context, there are already examples of successful use of ai in criminal cases, which have led to a more efficient and fairer system. take the prison reform lawsuit in the state of new jersey.
mr president, mr vitanov's report on the use of artificial intelligence in criminal proceedings is good, balanced and professionally drafted. so i think the good part is that it shows the positive side of ai in criminal justice, and it shows the negative side. in my opinion, as a long-time judge, i think that you have to be careful about using artificial intelligence in the decision-making process of a court. there should be a human element to it, because the decision about guilt and punishment is always individual, and no algorithm can possibly apply that to a given situation. even in this room, the president is making decisions now and then, he has to make decisions, and if he didn't, there would be artificial intelligence sitting here instead of the president, and there would be artificial intelligence sitting here instead of meps, because we would know how to make decisions based on algorithms. the potential for misuse of ai in the protection of human rights and freedoms should be avoided, but in principle and in principle, ai is a contribution to the fight against crime and should be encouraged, should be developed.

member of the commission. madam president, mr president of the special committee on foreign interference in the european union's democratic processes as a whole, including disinformation, mr glucksmann, ladies and gentlemen, first of all, i would like to welcome the opportunity to present today through the answers to the five questions, which i will of course answer in a moment an overview of all our efforts. we really share the commission's determination to combat disinformation, the manipulation of information and interference in our democratic processes - and you have spoken very highly of this. since 2017, the commission has been working to strengthen the capacity of our societies to understand, detect and counter the threats posed by disinformation. of course, i want to be as clear as you were: these hybrid threats, whether they come from third-party actors or insiders, are actually using platforms as a proliferation agent to, you name it, destabilize our societies. we have adopted several initiatives that guide our ambitions. i would like to remind them, if i may, of the 2018 action plan against disinformation; today, the action plan for european democracy, which was adopted at the end of last year; the commission has already proposed legislation on several aspects, or will soon do so. if platforms improve the speed of information, they obviously facilitate the same speed of disinformation, as you all know. i therefore call on them to take decisive action, in line with their responsibilities, to put a stop to this phenomenon. and i've interacted with them a lot, and i've actually told them all this many, many times, collectively and individually. in answer to your five questions, mr president, let me first recall the framework and structures of coordinated action, before returning to some specific initiatives. let us now turn to the first two questions you asked about increased cooperation and coordination and the link with the member states. i would remind you that our mechanisms of institutional cooperation and coordination at the level of the european union and with the member states have been strengthened in recent years, in particular thanks to the early warning system which is under the responsibility, as you know, of the european external action service. to this end, the authorities of the member states and the commission regularly exchange views on the threats linked to disinformation, manipulation and foreign interference. it is now also facilitating such exchanges with our international partners, be it the g7 or nato, and we are also working closely with the eeas - you guessed it - on a protocol to further strengthen cooperation structures. the commission has therefore set up an internal network, bringing together different services, in order to be able to define a rapid and appropriate response. the commission and the eeas are looking at how to further broaden the range of possible interventions, such as our toolkit to combat foreign interference, as i speak to you. options such as public attribution of disinformation campaigns are also on the table. i would like to turn now to your third question on the implementation of the legislative proposals and the associated codes of conduct. as you know, our proposed digital services legislation introduces harmonised rules to combat illegal content, in particular hate speech. it also creates obligations that can change the game in terms of disinformation, for example by ensuring that users are properly informed about the origin of advertisements that appear online, or that they can set the parameters of recommendation systems that often determine content. the very large platforms will also be required to assess and address systemic risks, including the intentional manipulation of their services with an impact on health, civic discourse, electoral processes and, more generally, public security. one way to mitigate these risks will be to adhere to these codes of conduct. in this way, legislation will create a kind of safety net, if one can say so, which is co-regulated. the commission is currently facilitating the work of the signatories of the code and our expectations are very clear: they were spelled out in our guidelines of last may and we therefore want a robust code by the end of this year, with verifiable and uniform commitments, of course, in all member states and in all the languages of the union. this concerns a number of areas: monetisation of disinformation, intensification of efforts against manipulative behaviour, but also software robots, so-called bots, and increased cooperation with the research community, which must have constant access to this information, because they are doing absolutely essential work. let us now turn to the question of algorithmic transparency and political advertising, which you raised in your fourth question. as you say, the question is of course: it is a very important point that the transparency of the algorithms. the legislation on digital services introduces several measures in this regard around algorithmic content moderation and recommendation or advertising systems. i would like to make it clear to you, ladies and gentlemen: it is absolutely intolerable that algorithms - as we see too often - promote the virality of disinformation content to create traffic, and thus monetisation. this monetisation must end, of course, and with the legislation on digital services and other actions under way, we will provide the means. we also naturally share all your concerns about the collection and combination of personal data from various sources. that's why the digital marketplace legislation this time around prohibits access controllers, gatekeepers, from combining personal data collected from their platform services with other services. finally, as regards transparency in political advertising, the commission will shortly present a specific legislative proposal, because the challenges to be addressed are very numerous and very important. these include political micro-targeting, behavioural profiling based on incorrectly obtained personal data - there are many examples, unfortunately - and misrepresentation of the funding and origin of political messages. your fifth question concerns media education and media diversity, if i may summarize it this way. the role of media literacy and a diverse media ecosystem is absolutely essential. i think you really have to learn it at a young age. these are fundamental pillars of our resilience, which the commission will continue to promote through various programmes and initiatives. we are funding a number of media education actions to this end. at national level, a dedicated expert group works closely with the member states. the commission shall, by autumn 2022, develop common guidelines for teachers to combat disinformation as part of the digital education action plan. support for a diverse and high-quality media landscape obviously involves project funding, but not only. information is a public good, and journalists are the guarantors of it, and they should not be its victims. to make their work safer, my colleague vera jourová and i recently proposed a recommendation to member states to take measures to make the work of journalists safer, both online and offline. unfortunately, there are tragic examples in both cases. other initiatives will follow, including to protect journalists and human rights defenders from abusive litigation, and to increase transparency, independence and accountability for actions affecting the control and freedom of the press. this is what we are trying to do in the media freedom legislation. ladies and gentlemen, the union is making progress on several fronts in the fight against disinformation, and our ability to implement a coherent set of actions is based on effective cooperation at the political level, but also at the operational level. the commission is fully committed to this and i know, of course, that we can count on your support. but i couldn't talk about platforms without talking about the news surrounding one of them. first of all, mrs haugen's revelations about the practices of a platform are important. they are not surprising, however. i said it at the beginning of the year: we can't let a private company decide for itself who can speak and who can be silenced, what can be seen and what should be removed or simply hidden, all because of a mysterious algorithm and without any democratic control process. the purpose of the digital services act is precisely to open up this black box, to restore democratic legitimacy and to ensure that the pursuit of profit does not simply endanger our democratic societies and our children. the platforms, we keep saying so we're going to work on this through the digital services and digital markets legislation have a very strong societal responsibility on the system actors. as for the incident that affected all services on the platform last night, although a network failure can obviously happen to anyone in the digital and computing space, this incident also highlights that resilience is key. billions of users and millions of businesses depend on this infrastructure. because of their size and their strategy of integrating services, some platforms have become, so to speak, too big to crash. our role is to support greater digital resilience through diversification of all actors, regulation, fair competition and also, we come back to it, technological and technical aspects, greater connectivity, redundancy in connectivity, but also a greater interest in everything related to cybersecurity.

for the ecr group. the institutions of the banking union have moved on from the financial crisis with reforms improving its stability and resilience. however, the proposal to establish a common deposit guarantee under current conditions would still, in my view, constitute a moral hazard, by shifting the potential costs from one group of euro area members to another. we have not yet exhausted the possibilities for improving the health of the banking sector without additional guarantee schemes. domestic tasks in the form of structural reforms and reducing the level of non-performing loans have still not been met by several euro area members. shared risk must be preceded by clear conditions for reforms and their implementation in such a way as to avoid fiscal black-mail. otherwise, the joint guarantee would be an unfair zero-sum game, where not everyone wins, but everyone loses.

in writing. it is our duty at eu level to do everything we can to ensure that the food our citizens, and especially children, consume is not only healthy, safe, but also affordable. ideally, as much of the food we eat should be produced at home, or at least within the eu, in the most environmentally and climate-friendly way possible. without real changes in the way food is produced, nature and the climate will suffer more and more. this is why the new 'from farm to fork' strategy is certainly welcomed by everyone, with the aim of reducing the use of pesticides and fertilisers and increasing the scale of organic farming. but we must do so without causing the devastating declines in meat, cereal and even fruit production, or the appreciable reduction in farmers' incomes that researchers at the eu's joint research centre have also warned would threaten the implementation of the proposed strategy. the reduced production in the eu would certainly be replaced by imports of less organic food from third countries, which would not only worsen the eu's trade balance but also jeopardise our objective of reducing greenhouse gas emissions from agricultural activity. in short, the strategy sets itself noble objectives, the achievement of which must be further supported by concrete measures to mitigate any possible negative impact on farmers and consumers.

rapporteur. – madam president, methane is a powerful greenhouse gas, warming the planet 80 times as much as carbon dioxide over a 20-year period before degrading to co2. we should provide a fair, comprehensive and clear legislative framework, setting binding measures and methane reduction targets covering all sectors, leading to a significant reduction of methane emissions in the eu by 2030. ahead of cop26, a challenge is arising. by concluding a global agreement on reducing methane emissions, we can ensure a new era of fast-track reduction of greenhouse gas emissions. after the announcement by the president of the commission, ursula von der leyen, and the us president, joe biden, of the global methane pledge – an initiative to reduce global methane emissions to be launched at the un climate conference in glasgow in november – we have to start working intensively in order to prepare the legislative framework as soon as possible, and to engage all relevant stakeholders. more than half of global methane emissions stem from human activities in three sectors: energy, waste and agriculture. in this framework, it is important to proceed with an ambitious revision of our environmental legislation. the upcoming methane regulatory measures should strive to achieve significant emissions reductions, swiftly and as cost-effectively as possible, and provide incentives and support to companies to achieve performance standards in an optimal manner, while fully respecting the ‘polluter pays’ principle. in the energy sector, inputs account for over four-fifths of the oil and gas consumed in the eu, and most methane emissions associated with oil and gas are occurring outside eu borders. that’s why we must explore regulatory tools on fossil energy inputs, develop methods with importing and partner countries to align our efforts, and secure a un-based pathway on methane as soon as possible. in the meantime, we could proceed with bilateral agreements with these exporting partner countries. it is necessary also to provide credible data, identify issues and efficient measures and assess the progress achieved. a mandatory mrv (measurement, reporting and verification) system would improve member states’ reporting to the un framework convention on climate change, and a strong leak and detection repair system is also a critical element for our strategy. we should encourage the commission to proceed with initiatives on the mitigation of methane from coal mines. it is important for us to include the closure of abandoned sites. in greece, we have western macedonia and the megalopolis lignite centre. it is important, finally, to ensure that we provide financial aid so that the cost will not be paid by our citizens. we also have to support the establishment of an independent international methane emissions observatory, in partnership with the united nations developmental programme and the climate and clean energy coalition under the international energy agency (iea). at the same time, we need to ensure a just transition for sectors in which methane emissions reductions may have socio-economic impacts, especially for agriculture. we have to incentivise farmers and smes to adapt, provided that a lot of innovative best practices are in place. it is important to establish a framework that incentivises and rewards farmers, along with the entire value chain, and especially front runners for their efforts. in the waste sector, we should support member states and regions to stabilise biodegradable waste prior to disposal, increase its use to produce climate neutral, secure and bio-based materials and chemicals, and divert this waste towards bio-gas production. we must provide specific incentives suited to each member state’s conditions to ensure separate collection of bio-waste, to the maximum possible extent, including by encouraging public private sector cooperation. to conclude, i would like to thank all my colleagues and the shadow rapporteur for the collaboration and support during the negotiation process. we have managed to finalise a very balanced report and to send the right message to our citizens: by reducing methane now, we will prevent nearly 0.3 degrees celsius of warming by 2045. the immediate implementation of methane reduction measures for human sources of methane could reduce methane emissions by as much as 45% by 2030. as the president of the commission declared, on the road to cop26, we will reach out to global partners to bring as many as possible on board for tackling methane emissions with the eu green deal and the eu methane strategy, we are ready to lead the way.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on industry, research and energy. – madam president, i would like to thank the rapporteur and congratulate ms spyraki on the very good, very balanced, report and for the good cooperation with the committee on industry, research and energy (itre). i also congratulate commissioner kadri simson on the good new strategy to reduce methane emissions. we need to strengthen the business case for capturing methane emissions. the main points for itre: highlighting the role of gas in energy transition and also highlighting the work done so far by the gas industry; leak detection and repair (ldar) and measurement, reporting and verification (mrv) as the key priorities to ensure better understanding of methane emissions to better data and more adequate reduction of emissions; compulsory mrv and ldar framework that also includes imports in order to have an equal playing field for the industry. we welcome the commission’s consideration of rules banning routine venting. we call for stronger measures and incentives for methane emissions in coalmines in a specific programme to address methane emissions from abandoned and closed coalmines. and finally, recognising the role of infrastructure operators in tackling methane leaks and calling for incentives.
member of the commission. – madam president, i would like to thank honourable members for inviting me to this timely discussion. the commission welcomes the parliament’s own-initiative report on our methane strategy prepared by ms spyraki and its recommendations. many of them are in line with, and endorse, the actions set out in the commission’s strategy. methane has accounted for roughly 30% of global warming since pre—industrial times and is proliferating faster than at any other time since record—keeping began in the 80s. the most rapid and cost effective methane emissions savings can be achieved in the energy sector. this is why we are preparing a legislative proposal to reduce methane emissions in the energy sector for adoption in december this year. it will rest on the following key pillars: improve the accuracy of information on the exact amounts and main sources of methane emissions to allow for more effective and more targeted methane abatement measures, and achieve immediate emission reductions across the energy supply chain by intervening on those fronts where action is possible. this includes mandatory leak detection and repair and limiting venting and flaring. we are also exploring the possibility to incentivise methane emission reductions outside the eu from our trading partners. the eu is a global fossil fuel importer and must use this leverage to foster methane emissions commitments among its suppliers. this can only be a progressive process, and we will work on further measures once more accurate and reliable data becomes available. i also want to underline that, alongside this proposal, we have been working with our energy partners and other key fossil importing countries to tackle methane emissions globally. thanks to eu energy diplomacy and partnership with the united nations environment programme (unep), in october 2020, the independent international methane emissions observatory (imeo) was created. furthermore, in september 2021, the union and the united states announced the global methane pledge, which represents a political commitment to reduce collectively global methane emissions by 30% by 2030. so far, 36 countries have committed their support, representing one third of total world methane emissions. many more are expected to join. the pledge will be launched at the cop26 in glasgow. the european parliament report also covers environmental legislation. we are now reviewing these parts and the options set out in the report in the ongoing impact assessments. moreover, the revision of the industrial emissions directive (ied) will look into measures such as methane emissions from relevant sectors – agriculture, industry and energy – whilst ensuring that there is no overlap in regulation. agriculture also has a significant potential to deliver on methane emissions savings. a range of mitigation technologies and practices are available. these are mainly related to the improvement of animal diets, herd management, manure management and soil protection, breeding, herd health and animal welfare. the current common agricultural policy (cap) is supporting mitigation actions for methane reduction in the livestock sector. in the post—2020 cap, the higher level of flexibility will give member states the possibility to design a specific combination of interventions for reducing emissions from agriculture. the commission is encouraging member states to include methane reduction schemes in their strategic plans for the cap. a significant share of global methane emissions in the agriculture sector originates outside of the eu. the commission and the member states have been very active in various international fora for reducing emissions from agriculture and agri—food systems. in conclusion, i welcome the spyraki report and take it as an encouragement for the commission to move forward with these bold and concrete legislative measures. i am looking forward to continuing the excellent cooperation with the parliament on this topic.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals. first of all, i would like to thank mrs spyraki and congratulate her on her report. methane is a huge problem: it's the second most important greenhouse gas. even though it has a shorter lifespan than co2, it has a much higher greenhouse gas potential. the next ten years, we don't know exactly, but the next ten years will be crucial to avoid dangerous tipping points in the climate system, such as the thawing of the permafrost in siberia. so too much methane in the next decade can mean very, very much methane for our children and our grandchildren, and then we can't control climate change. but i am very much in favor of not making this a fight between climate change and agriculture. farmers are part of the solution. they also offer many lowlands, for example through the cultivation of humus or sustainable forestry. that is why we should not stigmatize them, but work with farmers to reduce methane emissions. we should prevent leaks in the gas sector and we should finally ensure that no waste ends up in landfills in europe. this has to stop, because it's also producing methane, and we're destroying important resources that our children still need. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. climate change is not a national or purely european problem. we need international cooperation, and we need to keep going.
the s&d group is calling for the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the eu's climate action plan is implemented. methane is the second largest contributor to climate change after co2, accounting for more than 10% of total greenhouse gas emissions in europe, and contributes to the formation of ozone in the lower atmosphere, a potent air pollutant that causes serious health problems. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mrs spyraki, and the other honourable members for the work they have done to produce this report, in which we are asking the commission to ensure that the european union's strategy for reducing methane emissions includes the creation of a comprehensive legislative framework on methane emissions, with binding measures in all sectors - energy, recycling, agriculture and livestock in particular - the imposition of import control measures on emissions throughout the value chain - the european union leads the control policies but must impose them on its partners abroad - and the prohibition of practices such as venting and burning, as well as the commitment to the conversion of agricultural and livestock waste into biogas and the involvement of member states by introducing effective and sustainable measures in their national strategic plans, which must be strictly monitored by the commission. the commission should incorporate these and other measures in this report into its legislative proposal for reducing methane emissions in the energy sector, which it will present in december. we cannot fail to commit ourselves to making europe the first climate-neutral continent by 2050, to implementing the european green deal and to contributing to the well-being and health of citizens today and for future generations.
on behalf of the renew group. – madam president, we have to do the utmost to limit the global warming to 1.5 degrees. we got a taste this summer and honestly, i think we are standing on the brink of a climate disaster. but there is no chance to achieve this if we don’t act on methane, if we don’t properly act on methane, and act now, because honestly, we have overlooked it way too much. i’m not going to repeat the words of maria spyraki about potency and the timeline, but it’s clear that we have something on our hands where we clearly need to act. sadly, we don’t really even have a proper overview. we see the releases of documentations about the previously unknown leaks all around the world, and this is something which needs to call us even more urgently to action. we need to act above all in the energy sector. mrv and ldar – these might be shortcuts, but they are really important words. we need to ban routine flaring and venting, and we need to act at home and abroad, because not just climate has no boundaries, but most of our footprint is abroad. and it will provide a level playing field for european businesses. we have to look at our agriculture – not just the cows’ diet, our diet; together with the farmers, not against the farmers. we have to solve the landfills with circular economy, and we have to take methane to the global stage in glasgow. i hope that we will see some proper action led by europe there as well. madam commissioner, i hope that what we see from you is soon also an ambitious legislation that will not only deliver for this house but for our future.

for the ecr group. mr president, i would like to thank and congratulate the rapporteur, mrs charanz, for the quality of her work. the result is also consistent with this, and i wholeheartedly support it. the new rules on compulsory insurance will bring several changes, but also an important role for the member states. if a citizen living in, for example, slovakia or another member state bought a vehicle in, for example, germany, he had to insure it there for transport. after the new one, he'll just have to insure it in slovakia. if you drive without an accident and you come to a member state where those who drive without an accident have cheaper insurance, you will be entitled to get it too, like the slovak, the czech, the hungarian, the pole. the requirements for insuring vehicles will also change. electric bicycles and garden tractors will no longer require insurance. a fast electric skateboard with a speed of over 15 miles per hour is now a new one. however, member states were also given the possibility of derogations. for example, not requiring insurance for vehicles that only drive on private land, as the risk of causing harm to someone in this way may be low. it will be for the member states to consider how to set up exceptions so that we can rely on the guarantee fund only for damage that is worth relying on.

in writing. i am pleased that, after more than five years since the panama papers scandal broke and the commission published its proposal for this directive, an agreement has finally been reached between the council and the ep, which will increase the tax transparency of large companies. it's something that's been needed for a long time. this is undoubtedly of great importance, because for too long the eu has been watching as large corporations exploit the lack of common rules. the adoption of this agreement is an important element in the fight against international tax evasion and avoidance, and increases the tax transparency that is essential to achieve tax justice. the directive will put an end to the ability of large multinationals to hide and will require them to disclose tax information, including where they make their profits, where and how much they pay taxes, their turnover and their losses. i also think it is important because tax havens are also in the eu and in this way we get information about the individual member states and the situation in them. of course, disclosure alone will not completely solve the problem of tax evasion, but it is a step in the right direction.

the rapporteur. madam president, political parties are indispensable pillars of a pluralistic democracy. free to organise themselves, free to intervene in the political debate and to animate it, they structure the political landscape while highlighting their specificity and diversity. their freedom of expression - it has become commonplace to repeat it - should not be restricted to the point of stifling political debate. our european model is built on the irreducibility of the dignity of the person. the individual who breaks this rule is in conflict with the law, but it is not clear why this rule should not apply to parties as well. this report informs the commission of parliament's wish to enable european parties and foundations to concentrate on their political mission. to this end, it abolishes the requirement for parties to publish their accounts in ifrs, an obligation which would not meet any need and has never met any need. the report also opens the door to parties active in countries outside the european union. parties from the council of europe member states may, if they so wish, join european parties. we therefore call on the commission to include in its legislative proposal the possibility for parties to also participate financially in a referendum campaign in a member state where the issue of that campaign impacts the functioning of the european union, in particular. this is, of course, just one example among many, reflecting the rapporteur's desire to make existing provisions and regulations more flexible. as regards the funding of european parties and foundations, i am not going to hide from you that as rapporteur, my clear preference is for the model of financial support to parties based on the number of members of the european parliament who sit under the banner of their party. finally, to limit myself to the essentials, i would point out that the report refers to transnational lists, which will of course only become a reality if there is the political will to do so. i would not like to conclude without a friendly thought for my co-rapporteur colleague rainer wieland, with whom i agreed on almost everything, and my thanks, of course, also go to my team.
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i would like to thank mr goerens in return, and i do not want to give the impression that we have a great deal of disagreement. i think we pulled on the same string, even at the same end and in the same direction. but i will not hide the fact that i would have liked to go a little further, and i will try to show that. i do not want to focus on the 98% of the report where we agreed not only between the rapporteurs but also across most of the parties. i want to deal with what is perhaps still controversial now and what will be decided by a quarrel later on. there is the question of the logo and the question of transnational lists - both important questions. but we believe that this is not part of the funding debate, but part of the voting rights debate. and i think the house in general always tends to leave scent marks everywhere, even where they might not belong. secondly, we want to strengthen the formation of political party families in europe as an important part of visibility, as an important part of a european public and therefore also as an important part of what we must succeed in doing so. we want the citizens to see that there is an election campaign and that there are differences between the parties, and we want to put fewer chains on the parties. i would like to see something more, however, that we tackle the abuses we have had in the existing legislation and that we also open up more opportunities to parties that are starting up, or that we also help parties that do have votes, perhaps even a considerable number of votes, but no mandates. we have abuses. we have seen in the past that members of parliament have joined forces on the basis of mandates, formed parties and acted under the motto 'take up arms and run!' and then, when parliament said, 'we want money back,' there was no one left to hold accountable. i think the right approach is to rely on votes and that the votes that have been acquired in party families, that they are also a measure of support. and when a party changes, a party family, the votes go with it. but we shouldn't be doing this on a mandate basis anymore, because there's too much room for abuse. i also believe that we should honour those who run in the european elections, join a european party family. and if you think about it, in a country where you have six or seven mps, you need more than 10 percent of the vote to even get a mandate. i think we should also honour the votes from those countries which did not lead to a mandate and not continue to support the closed job that we are often accused of. unfortunately, the groups which are usually vocal against the closed job are not in agreement. and i hope that this debate will also provide reasons why we should support the opening of the supposedly closed job, which is always lamented, why we should support the legislation on the key points, which will of course apply from 2024, why we should not support it.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, democracy is one of the fundamental values on which the european union is founded. european political parties and the foundations linked to them fulfil an essential role in representative european democracy. they create a direct link between the citizens and the political system, thereby enhancing the legitimacy of the system itself. a healthy european democracy needs well—informed and engaged citizens. strong european political parties and foundations that can foster political debate across europe are essential for creating a truly european public sphere. the revision of the rules applying to the european political parties and foundations is one of the tangible deliverables of the european democracy action plan adopted by the commission at the end of last year. from the very early phases of our preparatory work for this revision, the commission has endeavoured to consult all relevant stakeholders to ensure that their analysis and ideas can be considered. i personally engaged in exchanges with the leaders of european political parties to hear what are the challenges to the existing situation and what can be improved. we have aligned the timeline of our work on this file with the timeline of the parliament’s own—initiative evaluation report on the implementation of regulation (eu, euratom) no 1141/2014, so that we can also take the views of this house into account. the adoption of the commission’s evaluation report on the regulation is also in its final stage at this moment. our plan is to accompany the evaluation report with a legislative proposal for the recast of the regulation on european political parties and foundations to address loopholes and suggest improvements. the proposal will be part of a broader package of initiatives on democratic life and electoral processes in the eu, notably on the transparency of paid political advertising, which is also key for european political parties. the package is currently scheduled for adoption on 23 november. our aim is to ensure early adoption of the regulation to be in place in time for the european elections in 2024. let me stress that the commission agrees with the main findings of the parliament’s report, namely that the regulation provides a useful legal framework for the functioning of european political parties and their foundations, while a number of shortcomings hinder them from achieving their full potential as active players in european democracy. the co—rapporteurs, vice—president wieland and mr goerens, whom i met on several occasions as well as other members of the committee on constitutional affairs, have documented a number of critical issues and made proposals, for instance, on the respect for eu fundamental values, the need for own resources to improve the financial viability of european political parties and foundations, the possibility for using eu funds in national referendum campaigns linked to the implementation of the treaties and the possibility of receiving contributions from members outside the european union, to mention several main findings. i can assure you that the commission intends to take on board a number of key elements of your report. the commission’s proposal will also aim to clarify the cross—european character of european parties campaigning to avoid difficulties of the past. i would like to thank the co—rapporteurs and the committee on constitutional affairs. we have had meaningful cooperation and fruitful exchanges in the course of our work, and i can assure you that the commission is considering seriously your findings and recommendations. and now i look forward to our debate.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, european democracy must be more resilient, especially in this era when we are facing increasing foreign interference in our democratic processes, including through targeted and well—orchestrated disinformation campaigns that are often supported from abroad through illicit means and aimed at undermining our democratic fabric. this is an important resolution, dear colleagues, with a number of points that can make political parties in europe stronger and thus make democratic life of europe both richer and more secure. however, we have to get the specific steps right. first and foremost, we must focus on making a better link between our voters and europe. let us be careful as we finalise this resolution. there is no single pan—european constituency in the eu. if we want to turn the european union into a fully—fledged representative democracy, we must respect its dual nature as the union of states and union of citizens. geographical representation next to ideological representation is crucial. any institutional steps that run against the principle of territorial representation and would put smaller member states candidates at a competitive disadvantage compared to those of larger member states would weaken democracy in europe. second, we must increase the engagement of voters. when i look at the levels of turnout in european elections across central europe, we have a deep problem with low participation of voters. the key here is to make better links between european parties and national parties, including through votes—based funding schemes for political parties in european elections. we must work on a more pluralistic political environment at european level, and this resolution suggests a solid way forward.
author. – mr president, commissioner schmit, dear colleagues, it is a great honour to open this plenary session with the committee on employment and social affairs (empl committee) oral question on the european social security pass, since this is a topic that concerns millions of mobile citizens and workers within the european union. the european parliament has been calling on the european commission since 2014 to present a proposal for a european social security number so that we can facilitate the mobility of workers, combat social fraud and simplify administrative procedures. the commission had announced in its work programme for 2018 that it would come up with a legislative proposal on such a european social security number, but in early 2021 we learned that this project was abandoned, which came as a surprise to us. instead, the commission announced that it would launch a pilot project to explore launching a digital european social security pass by 2023. so this question for oral answer and the draft motion for a resolution which will be put to the vote on wednesday: we would like to get more information about the social security pass and understand why the commission abandoned the project of launching a european social security number. allow me, therefore, to ask you the following questions. first of all, for parliament it is very important to know and understand the reasons why the commission abandoned working on the european social security number and replaced it instead with a pilot project on a digital pass. on several occasions this year, a member of the committee for employment and social affairs therefore asked the commission to give parliament access to all relevant documents and evidence on which it had based its decision to discontinue the exploration and introduction of an essn. i’m very pleased to announce that, in anticipation of today’s oral question and the debate, the commission reacted to these repeated requests for transparency and explanations, and at the end of last week, i actually received a letter, together with the relevant internal documents and evidence that will allow us to better understand why the project of introducing a european social security number was abandoned. my first question, in which i wanted to ask the commission to give parliament access to all relevant documents and evidence on which it has based its decision to discontinue the exploration of introducing an essn, has thus already been answered. but i would still be grateful if the commission could explain again the reasons why the european social security number was replaced with the digital pass. my second question is related to timing. earlier this year in the european pillar of social rights action plan, the commission announced that it would launch a pilot project to explore launching a european social security pass by 2023. we believe that a legislative proposal for such a pass is urgently needed. therefore, we would like to ask the commission if the timeline for the proposal can be moved from 2023 to 2022, to improve the chances of reaching an agreement within the current legislature. my third question is related to real-time verification, which is a very important point for the european parliament. can the commission explain how it will ensure that the social security pass will enable real-time verification of workers’ place of work, place of employment, employment relationship, identity and standardised social security benefits provisions and relevant certificates, as set out in regulation 883 on the coordination of social security systems? my fourth question: it is also very important for us that the european social security pass covers all mobile citizens and workers. can the commission confirm that the pass will indeed cover all mobile citizens and workers, including those ones who are self-employed, as well as mobile third-country nationals? in some member states, national cards or comparable instruments for labour law purposes exist already, and we ask ourselves how they will be linked to the european social security pass once created. can the commission explain how it plans to investigate the feasibility of allowing member states to use the european social security pass to draw on information made available from national cards or comparable instruments for labour law purposes where they already exist? can the commission also commit to assessing which other areas of eu labour law could be included within the scope of the european social security pass? data protection: it’s another question – another very important issue for the european parliament – so i would be grateful if you could explain how the commission will make sure that the eu data protection rules are strictly applied so that data is only available to the worker and the national authorities concerned. my last question is about the connection between the european social security pass and other tools and initiatives. there are already several initiatives and digitalisation processes at european level which concern the area of social security coordination, such as prior notification for posted workers, etc. can the commission explain how these digital processes and tools will relate to the european social security pass?
in writing. the free movement of workers is one of the fundamental principles of the european union, enshrined in the treaty on the functioning of the eu, is one of the benefits that eu citizens value most, and also creates growth and prosperity within the eu beyond the borders of individual member states. but despite all the benefits it brings, we are still not in a situation where the social rights of mobile workers are really guaranteed. and that is a fundamental problem. member states and their social systems have a responsibility for the social security of people, but even today these systems cannot communicate with each other, which leads to violations of workers' rights and unfair competition. the european parliament has been raising this issue for years, but without any concrete results. the commission has reportedly decided to abandon the legislative proposal to create a european social security number and instead plans to submit a proposal to create a digital european social security card. the name of this instrument is, in my view, secondary; it is essential that the commission submits its proposal as soon as possible. the proper protection of the social rights of mobile workers must become a reality and we must have the tools to effectively combat cross-border social fraud by companies. citizens must be able to rely on working in different member states to ensure that they do not lose their social security rights.
in writing. one of the european union's biggest challenges for the coming decades is to reduce our dependence on imported minerals, which we need to develop cleaner technologies such as batteries, hydrogen or photovoltaics. today, almost 100% of our rare earths are imported from china, almost 80% of our lithium is imported from chile and 70% of our cobalt is imported from congo. indeed, it can be said that most strategic raw materials come to europe from countries in asia, africa and latin america that are unable to guarantee that their extraction and processing are carried out in a sustainable manner, respecting environmental standards and human rights. it is therefore clear that this dependence must be reduced if we are to move further in achieving our strategic autonomy. but the proposed provisions threaten to impose a virtually blanket ban on the extraction of strategic minerals in the eu's vast protected areas by giving the communities living there veto power over the approval of mining projects. while it is clear that the protection of protected areas and the involvement of different communities in the decision-making process must be a priority, on the other hand, banning any extraction in such areas would be contrary to our efforts to reduce our dependence on china. a compromise solution must therefore be found that protects the rights of communities but also biodiversity, while allowing the union to develop domestic capacities in strategic sectors.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, let me begin by thanking tomasz frankowski for his adventurous work on this report. ladies and gentlemen, today we are witnessing the entrenchment of the elitism of rich families, rich, rich children, due to the lack of support for poor children in sports. sports are becoming more expensive and, unfortunately, unaffordable for poor children. i regret to say that many sports are not accessible to the poorest children in europe. i had dozens of meetings with poor parents who actually gave me the benefit of the doubt. the availability of sport is conditional on the family's financial well-being. in other words, hundreds of thousands of european families cannot afford to pay for their children's coaches, to buy their children's sports equipment. many parents are happy to provide at least the basic necessities for their children. however, there are exceptions from time to time. as it was a while ago in my country, slovakia. in the case of a poor girl from a roman settlement, her name is anna maria, who managed to win a competition in running, passing without any sports training or equipment. she even wore the plain ballet slippers she wore to school. her victory in the ballet class literally shocked many. and her victory is also a reminder to all of us of the lack of interest of institutions in poor children who have talent, but whose parents are unable to support it because of poverty. it's not just about one special anne marie. there are many anna maria's in ballerinas who deserve our support. i therefore believe that it is important to keep in mind the important element of inclusiveness when designing and building european sports policies, or rather we must create conditions for sport for the poorest children in europe.
madam president, in every crisis there are voices calling for attempts to resolve crises by isolationism and protectionism. similar proposals are now emerging in the public debate in the context of the crisis caused by the covid-19 pandemic. for example, this report on the eu pharmaceutical strategy proposes to relocate the production of medicines to europe and also to promote made in europe medicines. i think that just as in previous crises it finally became clear that protectionism is not the solution, it will be shown now. moving the entire supply chain to europe would significantly over-price production and limit specialisation, including in the area of scientific progress. the real solution, in my view, is to deepen openness, cooperation and competition, to break down barriers and diversify supply chains. instead of isolationism, which is what marketing sometimes calls self-sufficiency.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the spread of lies, misinformation and hoaxes on the internet, especially on social networks, is a threat that is changing the world. social networking sites are no longer just for making contacts, disseminating information, or even for entertainment. half-truths, lies that spread at an incredible rate around the world, destabilizing society and causing chaos. social media, on the one hand, generates enormous amounts of profit through the spread of half-truths and lies, and on the other hand, is de facto untouchable compared to traditional media. if any traditional medium today were to spread the information that the cure for killing any virus in the body is to drink or even to administer intravenous disinfectant, the next day that medium, the traditional medium, would be facing a lawsuit. but social media is not in danger of this. they spread information without any problems that seriously endangers the lives, health or property of ordinary people without facing any liability. it's time to stop talking about regulating today's unregulated social media and start acting before it's too late.
mr. president, there was a vote recently here that europeans should eat more insects instead of meat because meat/livestock is said to produce greenhouse gases that cause global warming. it was approved as part of the european food strategy from farm to fork, and as part of that, the european commission has already approved some two types of caterpillars or grasshoppers that people should eat. unfortunately, this agricultural policy is not much better, because there is also a lot of literal ecological nonsense and, in particular, this agricultural policy is unfair in the long term to the central and eastern european countries, which receive several times less support than western european farmers. slovakia was once a food-independent country. now they have to import 1,000 trucks of food a day to keep us fed. this too is the result of unfairly targeted subsidies. if you want to call this agricultural policy a common policy, first of all let us level the subsidies.

mr president, the reform of the common agricultural policy should be systemic and green. at the beginning of this process, i was really looking forward to it. unfortunately, at the end of this trial today, i'm rather disappointed. eco-schemes, climate targets, biodiversity support, support for small farmers, harmonisation of payments - all these powerful instruments have ultimately remained insufficient and formal, and we have been looking for measurable and binding targets in vain. unfortunately, we continue to support predominantly intensive agriculture, damaging the climate, the soil and biodiversity. the biggest recipients of subsidies will continue to be the big corporations and oligarchs, with no added value for diversity, local production or even transparency. the cap on subsidies that could have done something about this unbalanced system remains voluntary. the foundations of the reform were laid before the european environmental convention and unfortunately the reform has remained largely in this setting. it completely ignores and fails to refer to key strategies such as the biodiversity strategy and the farm-to-table strategy. we are living in a climate and biodiversity crisis. the reform should and could have made agriculture a system that is not the cause but the solution to this crisis. unfortunately, the principle attributes for this goal are missing in the reform.

for the ppe group. madam president, in a state governed by the rule of law, respect for human rights, or the fair and dignified treatment of a person, is a principle that everyone should follow. in europe, there's been a long debate about the adequacy of policing. i'll mention the cases i've been working on recently. the case of the abused boys from the police station in košice, slovakia, where the police forced roma boys to undress and then slap each other. the policemen in this case were acquitted and cleared by the slovak courts. another case of police raid, also from slovakia, from moldavia over bodva. this case was also dismissed in slovakia. the european court of justice was the only place where the citizens concerned could expect justice. i would also like to mention the recent case of a belgian airport police station against ján chovanc from slovakia, who died after being shot in the presence of a hailing policewoman. how much time we have, colleagues, devoted to the american george floyd and how much time we have devoted to these causes. friends, have you heard of the case of the young roma stanislav tomas from český teplice? he, like george floyd, dies after being kneeled on by the police. as is customary, the control authorities failed to investigate the police intervention in question. all the european cases i've mentioned here are known only from randomly generated videos. if it weren't for these videos, we wouldn't even be talking about these cases today. it's not just the control bodies that are failing, it's not just the black sheep of the police corps that are failing, but it's also the monitoring of the actions of the police officers that we have no control over. every european police officer must be equipped with a camera so that his or her intervention can be evaluated at any time.
