mr president, although the protection of climate and environment should not be subject to political discretion, the approved texts of cop26 are a compromise and reflect the political will of today. i however welcome the cop26 results, and in particular the pledges related to ecosystems. we so far have treated nature as the least important element of our fight to mitigate and adapt to climate change. as some think, when sacrificed should not have consequences. we replaced digging for coal by the burning of forests, started to call it a fight against climate change, and rewarded companies for burning wood despite the fact that wood seals more co2 per unit of energy than coal, and despite the very poor shape of our forests. we cannot afford to go down this line. we have to strictly go the path of energy efficiency through renewables like solar, wind and geothermal, together with forest and wetland protection, to really improve the climate. unfortunately, it is reported that the eu is proposing to expand the scope of the energy charter treaty and replace fossil fuels by new energy sources such as biomass. the cop27 starts now and we have to act now. that’s why i hope that this parliament is aware of the damage that the eu has cast upon forests with the renewable energy directive and is ready to review it thoroughly.
mr president, the conclusions of glasgow have not killed hope that our planet will no longer warm as rapidly and intensively as it has in the past. europe is responsible for only 8% of greenhouse gas emissions, while our biggest competitors china and the us are much more. it's not enough for them to announce ambitious goals, but we have to push them through climate diplomacy to put those goals into practice. the council in glasgow did not decide to take the revolutionary steps we recently did by adopting the green deal, which we are trying to transform into concrete steps through the fit for 55 package. some proposals are controversial and will not be easy to agree on. and it's going to be even harder to implement with the consent of the citizens. on the other hand, frans timmermans is right that if we do not decide to take radical steps today, then we risk not being able to change much later. we must do everything we can to keep our planet competitive, to avoid deepening energy poverty and the division of people into the privileged and the marginalized. only through social justice will we get more people on the green side of our economy, transportation and housing. people expect us to provide specific social guarantees. it can also be a climate social fund, but it needs to be more ambitious, we need to strengthen its financing not only through emission allowances, and we need to broaden its scope.
mr president, the cheapest energy is still the one we save and do not have to produce at all, and therefore our priority must be to increase energy efficiency. but let us be fair and admit that if the union imports raw materials from third countries whose production does not respect our green norms, or our social standards, we are only exporting emissions and contributing to their increase elsewhere. but the planet we live on is only one. at home, we have to respect the different starting positions of the different countries. in some, both gas and nuclear will have to be used for a few more years to decarbonise faster. of course, renewable sources must be given priority, with cross-border electricity transmission capacities to be strengthened in the future. the need to strengthen resilience and energy security is all the more urgent as rising energy prices threaten vulnerable households. while people understand the need for emissions reductions and green reforms, they will only support them if they see social guarantees, and so we need to be more ambitious in ensuring social justice and allocating more money to the climate social fund and paying for activities other than just some training or retraining.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, peace, justice, respect for every human being. when we're young, we think that these are taken for granted, that we all have these positive principles inside of us. but when you're an adult, you realize there's good and there's evil. today i want to use very simple words that are understandable to all who are listening. the istanbul convention is "the first legally binding international instrument that creates a comprehensive legal framework to protect women from all forms of violence". so the istanbul convention is a treaty, and treaties must first be signed and then ratified by the states, so that the states are bound to respect these treaties. in this convention we are talking about very simple concepts. it is said that states should include in their penal codes a number of crimes against women: psychological violence, sexual violence, stalking, physical violence, rape, forced marriage, female genital mutilation, forced abortion, forced sterilization, sexual harassment. the following are examples of such violence: the european union has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to take action in the fight against terrorism. european citizens, women and fathers, today you must ask your governments and your politicians who represent you: why? why? why is it so hard to face up to sexual violence or genital mutilation? why is it so hard to face up to forced marriage or rape? the task of politicians is to make decisions and represent democracy. i would like to know why certain countries in the european union are opposed to this treaty and whether these countries really represent the values of the european union.

mr. president, i will be speaking my mother tongue, in slovak, colleagues, 600, 600 women, over 600 women were murdered in 2018 by an intimate partner or family member or their relatives in 14 european union countries, according to the latest available data from eurostat, and this is before the pandemic. how many other women have been killed since then in other european union countries for which we have no data? how many women face physical and psychological violence every day? and how many reported cases never show up in this data? indeed, violence against women is one of the biggest problems we face as a democratic and modern society. in the union, we are very effective at protecting consumers and their rights, but we are not doing enough to protect women from physical and psychological violence. we pride ourselves on being a great regulatory power, but in many member states we have failed to ratify the istanbul convention. i therefore call on the commission to adopt urgently measures at eu level equivalent to the ratified convention.
mr president, the protection of women from violence and such protection must be considered the number one priority not only in legislation but above all in the minds of men. i am very familiar with the controversial and legally imperfect istanbul convention. as a judge with 40 years of experience, i am also very familiar with the legislation of the slovak republic. i must say, objectively and without emotion, that the legislation of the slovak republic, as far as the protection of women's rights against violence is concerned, is more perfect and better, more objective and uncomplicated. i therefore propose that the european union legislators should become familiar with the jurisdiction of the slovak republic, adopt and implement it into european union law and then we can safely throw the istanbul convention into the english channel. and one last thing. i object to the remarks of some members of the european parliament who claim that the countries which have not accepted the istanbul convention are somehow more backward. it's not like that in slovakia. we slovaks love our women because they are the most beautiful women in the world.

madam president, i am in favour of the regulation of social networks in the areas of protection of life, health, peace, protection of women's rights against violence, protection against pornography and so on. but i cannot agree with the regulation of social networks in the marketplace. in my opinion, it distorts the market, it distorts competition, it distorts what is the basis of the economy of any country, that is, the market economy. i also believe that regulation of networks in the market area will not be in the interest of the consumer. i believe that consumer protection must be ensured by strong legislation, by judicial legislation and, above all, by support for civil society organisations in the individual national states, which have a duty to deal with consumer protection. therefore, i cannot fully agree with the digital market act.
madam president, the main flaw in the proposed digital market act is the very definition of a gatekeeper. the size of the platform and the number of users do not in themselves demonstrate that there is a market failure. we should stick to the tried and tested procedures defined in the competition law, i.e. choose criteria that show whether a market failure is actually occurring. at the same time as the questions in dma, we should open a debate on why none of the current digital giants were born in the european union. enabling market entry is a better strategy than letting monopolies grow without competition and then regulating them. moreover, the proposed regulation also contains prohibitions and obligations for platforms that may be ineffective.

the next item is the debate on the commission statement on the eu-wide ban on the use of wild animals in circuses (2021/2999 ((rsp)). i would like to inform you that for all the debates of this part-session there will be no catch-the-eye procedure and that no blue-card questions will be accepted. as in recent sessions, there is also provision for remote intervention by the parliament's liaison offices in the member states. i would also remind you that the speeches in the chamber will continue from the main stage. so i would ask you to follow the list of speakers and come to the podium when your turn to speak is near.
member of the commission. – mr president, i have read the oral question from members across this house seeking a ban on the use of wild animals in circuses, and i appreciate your efforts in this regard. because the use of wild animals in circuses raises many issues of concern. while there are obvious concerns around animal welfare, this is principally an ethical question. on issues of ethics and where legislation is not harmonised at eu level, it is the member states that have the expertise, the understanding, and indeed the obligation, to take the necessary steps. and as a matter of fact, this is exactly what member states have been doing. indeed, as you point out in your question, the vast majority of them have already introduced a total or partial ban on the use of wild animals in circuses on their territory, and this reflects the public’s position on ethical and animal welfare grounds. animal and veterinary experts and bodies have supported these decisions. we welcome these measures and we invite those member states that do not yet have a ban in place to follow suit. to reiterate my point, we are of the opinion that any question on the banning of wild animals kept in circuses should not be decided at eu level but rather should be left to the member states concerned to regulate as a matter of conscience. this is in line with the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality, and the 23 national bans that are in place show us that these principles are being implemented in practice. that said, we do have rules at eu level that govern the movement of these animals between member states. to start with, movement of circus animals within the eu is implemented through the wildlife trade regulations. while our rules are based on the provisions of the convention on international trade in endangered species, in some respects in the eu we go further than the convention. generally speaking, wild animals may not be used for commercial purposes. moreover, importing them into and moving them around the eu is strictly regulated. in the case of circuses, the relevant certificates can only be issued to animals that have been legally acquired and have been born and bred in captivity. this makes it possible to carry out checks in the few remaining member states that still allow the use of wild animals in circuses and clearly limits the risk of illegal wildlife trade. secondly, the animal health legislation lays down rules for record keeping and movement for circus animals that move between the member states, and these rules are based on scientific risk assessments and conform to international standards. as such, they limit the risk of such movements on the animal’s health. honourable members, to wrap up, bans are a step for member states alone to take, and the vast majority of them have chosen to do so. on top of this, there is eu legislation to support member states as regards the movement of circus animals between them. we will continue to monitor the situation in this area, and i look forward to hearing your views on this important matter.
for the ppe group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, more than one million citizens of the european union have signed a petition calling for a ban on the use of wild animals in circuses. there's a certain tradition of entertainment, of visiting circuses. but i wonder if it is appropriate to show our children examples of tigers, lions or elephants performing various acrobatic feats that are not natural to them. you said it, commissioner. it's the ethical question. i am glad that most member states have solved this for us. 23 member states have adopted a ban on the use of wild animals in circuses. i would like to point out that there are other very pressing issues on the table at european level. the public is interested in how we can help them deal with the energy price hike, how we can get out of the covid crisis. in agriculture, we are waiting for strategic plans for the new agricultural policy. we have to address the impacts of climate change, the competitiveness of european farmers. i believe that it is not necessary at this stage to embark on the preparation of legislation to ban the use of wild animals in circuses throughout the european union. yet we take this voice of a million citizens seriously. the european union should pay strict attention to the welfare of animals. that is what we are going to do with the new common agricultural policy. all animals - domestic, farm and wild - deserve our attention, protection and respect for their natural needs. the european union has a 2019 regulation which imposes an obligation to carry out veterinary checks aimed at the movement of animals between member states. but in practice, it's not enforced enough, and there are problems. i believe that the application of existing standards needs to be improved, and this is a task for the member states and the european union. i would also like to ask the following question: how will the welfare of animals be checked when circuses from third countries come to perform? i think it is not possible to ban european circuses and still allow circuses from third countries to perform in our country without subjecting animal husbandry to the same strict rules. i therefore ask the commission to pay attention to these issues.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. wild animals in circuses don't belong in this century. it is time for the eu to listen to the more than one million citizens whose signatures have been submitted to the commission to ban this exploitation of animals. wild animals belong in the wild, not in the circus for our entertainment. captivity restricts the behaviour of wild animals and also poses risks to their physiological and psychological well-being. animals are usually trained hard, even when they are very young, in order to erase all their natural instincts and behaviors. by means of punishment and rewards, they learn to perform unnatural arts that are also harmful to the animals. a concrete example is the hind leg stand for elephants, which is very rarely performed in the wild and can result in joint damage. circus business is based on the fact that the business is on the move. the journey stresses the animals and their stay outdoors is often limited to small temporary enclosures for a short period of time. can you imagine the horror of animals traveling like that? of the eu member states, 24 have already adopted some form of restrictive measures against the use of wild animals in circuses. harmonised legislation in the eu prohibiting the transport of wild animals in circuses would avoid transporting animals between different member states with different levels of animal welfare. in fact, several countries around the world have already banned wild animals from circuses. there are animal-free circuses in malta, and cyprus has also come before. furthermore, bolivia and mexico have imposed a ban. other countries, such as belgium, austria, and iran, have banned wild animals from circuses. the traditional circus is actually outdated. fewer people are amused by the animals being transported around. instead, there is a renewed interest in so-called neo-circuses, in which humans perform in the place of animals. these amazing artists manage the cultural heritage of the circuses without harming the animals.
for the renew group. mr president, a friend once told me: 'seeing animals in the circus is part of childhood.' but children are not stupid. why do we assume that they'll think that a tiger is supposed to jump in a cage, that an elephant is supposed to jump between things in an arena, that a chimpanzee is supposed to ride a bicycle? kids really aren't stupid. and most europeans are not stupid either, because it's clear that most europeans think that wild animals don't belong in the circus. that they don't belong to the fun, that they don't suffer in training and then ten months a year ride locked in little maringots or cages and experience further suffering in performances. it is time we stopped saying that elephant, tiger, and other wildlife performances are entertainment. entertainment cannot be built on suffering and cruelty. i am glad that many countries, including slovakia, have taken up this challenge. and it is time for us to stand up to it as europe. we solve complicated things, we can solve simple ones. it's also important that it's not just about animal suffering. circuses are also, unfortunately, a source of endangered animals for the illegal trade. not only is the legislation lax and full of holes, but the very fact that wild animals can continue to be in circuses contributes to trade, for example, with tigers. it's something that's time to stop. not just because it threatens wildlife, but because of zoonotic diseases, like the one we're now cruelly reminded of by the corona. i therefore expect the commission to find legislative ways, it is not difficult, of finally banning wild animals in circuses throughout europe. because humanity towards animals is a european value.
